\mathbf{w}(s_{i})=\dot{\zeta}(s_{i}+)-\dot{\zeta}(s_{i}-),\quad\mathbf{v}=\frac{\dot{\zeta}(s_{i}+)+\dot{\zeta}(s_{i}-)}{|\dot{\zeta}(s_{i}+)+\dot{\zeta}(s_{i}-)|}

do the job.

Definition 2.7
.

A unit vector
\mathbf{v}
is called
supporting direction
in
\zeta(s)
if
\mathbf{v}
satisfies (
2.6
). The arc of the unit ball made of vectors satisfying (
2.6
) will be called
subdifferential of
\dot{\zeta}
, and denoted by
\partial^{-}\zeta(s)
.

Remark 2.8
.

The considerations above show that an equivalent definition is that
s\mapsto\dot{\zeta}(s)
is locally BV, and that (assuming that
\dot{\zeta}(s)
is right continuous)

D\dot{\zeta}(s)\wedge\dot{\zeta}(s)\geq 0.

If the above product is negative, then the rotation is clockwise.

We introduce now the rotation angle of the vector
\dot{\zeta}(s)
as the monotone increasing right continuous function
s\mapsto\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{varphitan}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi(s)}}
such that

\mathbf{t}^{+}(s)=e^{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{iimagi}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\imath}}\varphi(s)}.

This function is uniquely defined once we fix the initial angle, and in this paper we set

\varphi(0)=2\pi+\angle(\textbf{t}^{+}(0),\textbf{e}_{1}).

The interval
[0,2\pi)
will be used as the initial parametrization for the first rotation, see Proposition
2.11
.

We make the following additional assumption:

(2.7)

0\leq\angle(\textbf{t}^{+}(0),\textbf{e}_{1})\leq\frac{\pi}{2},

where
\textbf{e}_{1}
is the horizontal vector of the canonical base. This assumption is used to parametrize the spiral in polar coordinate for a first interval. By convention we assume that

\mathbf{t}^{-}(0)=\mathbf{e}_{1}=(1,0).

Remark 2.9
.

It is fairly easy to see that another equivalent definition to Definition
2.6
is that there exists such a monotone increasing function
s\mapsto\varphi(s)
such that
\varphi(s+)-\varphi(s-)<\pi
and
\dot{\zeta}(s)=e^{i\varphi(s)}
: in this case

\partial^{-}\zeta=\big\{e^{\imath\phi},\phi\in[\varphi(s-),\varphi(s+)]\big\}.

We can now give the definition of the class of barriers which we consider in this paper.

Definition 2.10
.

A single-barrier (not necessarily blocking) convex strategy satisfying Assumption (
2.7
) will be called a
spiral
. The set of
admissible spirals
(i.e. is the set of admissible single-barriers (not necessarily blocking) convex strategies satisfying assumption (
2.7
)) is denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{S}
.

2.3.1.
Angle-ray parametrization of spirals

From now on we will fix an admissible spiral
Z\in\mathcal{A}_{S}
. We will extend the alternative parametrization introduced first in
[
Kle19
]
for the self-similar saturated spiral (see Section
3.1
for definitions) to a general spiral strategy
Z
. We will call such a parametrization
angle-ray parametrization
or
angle-ray representation
.

The next proposition contains the core results of this section. In the statement the choice of the sequence of rounds is fixed (see Equation
2.9
): a simple adaptation of the proof allows some flexibility in the choice of the sequence of angles
\bar{\phi}_{\ell}
, see Remark
2.12
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\zeta
be a spiral strategy. Then there exist two sequences of real positive numbers

0\leq\bar{s}_{1}<\bar{s}_{2}<\dots<\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{skbar}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{s}_{k}}}<\bar{s}_{k+1}\leq S,\quad 0<\bar{\phi}_{1}=2\pi<\bar{\phi}_{2}<\dots<\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{phikbar}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{\phi}_{k}}}\leq\bar{\phi},

for some angle
\bar{\phi}
, with the following properties.

(1)

For
0\leq s<\bar{s}_{1}
the spiral
\zeta
coincides with the oriented segment

\zeta(s)=(1,0)+s\mathbf{e}_{1},

and the value
\bar{s}_{1}
is given by

\bar{s}_{1}=\inf\big\{\zeta^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}\mathbf{e}_{1})\big\}=\max\big\{s:\zeta([0,s])\subset\mathbb{R}\mathbf{e}_{1}\big\}.

(2)

For
0\leq\phi<\bar{\phi}_{1}=2\pi
the spiral can be represented in polar coordinates as

(2.8)

\zeta(s^{+}_{0}(\phi))=r_{0}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi},

with
r_{0}(\phi)
a Lipschitz function in
[0,2\pi]
. Defining the value

\bar{s}_{2}=\lim_{\phi\nearrow 2\pi}s^{+}_{0}(\phi)=\min\big\{s>\bar{s}_{1}:\zeta(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\mathbf{e}_{1}\big\}

as the value
s
of the next intersection with the line
\zeta(\bar{s}_{1})+\mathbf{e}_{1}\mathbb{R}^{+}=\zeta(\bar{s}_{1})+\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{1})\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the map (
2.8
) defines a continuous bijection

[0,2\pi]\ni\phi\mapsto s^{+}_{0}(\phi)\in[\bar{s}_{1},\bar{s}_{2}],

Lipschitz in
(\epsilon,2\pi]
for every
\epsilon>0
and with Lipschitz inverse, where the final value is
\bar{s}_{2}=s_{0}^{+}(2\pi)
.
The optimal rays are the segments
[(0,0),\zeta(\phi)]=r_{0}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}
, where
\zeta(\phi)=\zeta(s_{0}^{+}(\phi))
and the region
S_{1}
bounded by the Jordan curve
\zeta([\bar{s}_{1},\bar{s}_{2}))\cup[\zeta(\bar{s}_{2}),\zeta(\bar{s}_{1}))
has convex boundary apart from the point
\zeta(\bar{s}_{1})
and it is star-shaped w.r.t. the point
\zeta(\bar{s}_{1})
.

(3)

For
\ell=1,\dots,k-1
the angles
\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}
satisfy

(2.9)

\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}=2\pi+\bar{\phi}_{\ell}+\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{deltaphiell}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta\phi_{\ell}}},\quad\delta\phi_{\ell}=\angle(\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell+1}),e^{\imath\bar{\phi}_{\ell}})\in\bigg(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg),

and there exist three functions

[\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}]\ni\phi\mapsto(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{rellphi}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r_{\ell}(\phi)}},\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{s-ellphi}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi)}},\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{s+ellphi}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi)}})\in(0,\infty)\times[\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}]\times[\bar{s}_{\ell+1},\bar{s}_{\ell+2}],

with
s^{-}_{\ell}
increasing right continuous,
r_{\ell}(\phi)
right continuous one-sided Lipschitz with negative singular part of the derivative (i.e.
D^{\mathrm{sing}}r\leq 0
), and
s^{+}_{\ell}
Lipschitz increasing, bijective with Lipschitz inverse, such that

\zeta(s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi))=\zeta(s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi))+r_{\ell}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}.

The final point
\bar{s}_{\ell+2}
is given by

(2.10)

\bar{s}_{\ell+2}=\lim_{\phi\nearrow\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}}s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi)=\min\big\{s>\bar{s}_{\ell+1}:\zeta(s)\in\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})+\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})\mathbb{R}^{+}\big\},

Moreover the optimal ray ending in the point
\zeta(s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi))
is the union of the initial segment
[(0,0),(1,0))
, the spiral arc
\zeta([0,s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi)))
and the final segment
[\zeta(s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi)),\zeta(s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi))]=r_{\ell}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}
. Finally the boundary of the open bounded region

S_{\ell+1}
is the Jordan curve

\zeta([\bar{s}_{\ell+1},\bar{s}_{\ell+2}))\cup[\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+2}),\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1}))

which is convex outside the point
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})
and it is star shaped w.r.t. the point
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})
.

(4)

For
\phi\geq\bar{\phi}_{k}
there are three functions

[\bar{\phi}_{k},\bar{\phi}]\ni\phi\mapsto(r_{k}(\phi),s^{-}_{k}(\phi),s^{+}_{k}(\phi))\in[0,\infty)\times[\bar{s}_{k},\bar{s}_{k+1}]\times[\bar{s}_{k+1},S],

with
s^{-}_{k}
increasing right continuous,
r_{k}(\phi)
right continuous one-sided Lipschitz with negative singular part of the derivative and
s^{+}_{k}
Lipschitz increasing, bijective and with Lipschitz inverse, such that

\zeta(s^{+}_{k}(\phi))=\zeta(s^{-}_{k}(\phi))+r_{k}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}.

Moreover
r_{k}(\phi)=0
only if
\phi=\bar{\phi}
(and in this case
\zeta(S)\in\zeta([0,S))
,
and the optimal ray ending in the point
\zeta(s^{+}_{k}(\phi))
is the union of the initial segment
[(0,0),(1,0))
, the spiral arc
\zeta([0,s^{-}_{k}(\phi)))
and the final segment
[\zeta(s^{-}_{k}(\phi)),\zeta(s^{+}_{k}(\phi))]=r_{k}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}
.

We will refer to the decomposition of
[0,S]=\cup_{\ell}[\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}),[0,\bar{\phi}]=\cup_{\ell}[\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{p}hi_{\ell+1})
of the above proposition as the
decomposition in rounds
: indeed, Point (2) describes the first round about
{(0,0)}
, which in polar coordinates corresponds to a rotation of
2\pi
, Point (3) describes the next rotations of angles
>2\pi
about
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell})
, and the final Point (4) is the last part of the curve, which may close or remain open. For a picture of this representation, see Figure
12
.

Figure 12
.
Decomposition in rounds of any admissible spiral
Z
. The notation is the one of Proposition
2.11
. Observe that in the points of non-differentiability the angle is computed following the direction
\mathbf{t^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell})}

Proof.

The first two and the last cases can be seen as a particular case of Point (
3
) in the statement, hence we will only address it.

The iterative assumptions are that

(1)

the open region
S_{\ell}
, bounded by the Jordan curve
\zeta([\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}))\cup[\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1}),\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell}))
, is a star shaped region about
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell})
, with convex boundary outside the point
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell})
;

(2)

\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})\in\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell})+\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell})\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

These assumptions are clearly satisfied for
\ell=0
.

Step 1.
We first consider the barrier

\hat{\zeta}_{\ell}(s)=\begin{cases}\zeta(s)&s\in[\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}),\\
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})+\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})(s-\bar{s}_{\ell+1})&s\geq\bar{s}_{\ell+1},\end{cases}

obtained prolonging the tangent
\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})
indefinitely. A consequence of the analysis below is that if the half-line intersects the curve
\zeta([\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}))
, then we are in the situation of Point (4) of the statement, so it is not the case considered here: it is nevertheless easy to adapt the next steps to this last case.

We consider next the minimum time function for the new barrier
\hat{Z}_{\ell}=\hat{\zeta}([\bar{s}_{\ell},+\infty))
(see Fig.
13(a)
):

(2.11)

\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus S_{\ell}\ni x\mapsto u(x)=u(\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell}))+\inf\Big\{L(\gamma),\gamma(0)=\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell}),\gamma(1)=x\ \text{and}\ \gamma((0,1))\cap\hat{Z}_{\ell}=\emptyset\Big\}.

In the above representation we have observed that every optimal ray has to cross the segment
[\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell}),\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})]
, (
2.11
) gives the correct time function on the segment
[\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell}),\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})]
, and an optimal ray is a segment when not touching the barrier (because it must be of minimal length): hence it must pass through
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell})
.

(a)

The barrier for constructing
u(x)
, Equation
2.11
.

(b)

Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
2.11
.

Figure 13
.
Proof of Proposition
2.11
.

Elementary geometric considerations show that for every point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\clos\,S_{\ell}
there are at most
2
points
\zeta(s)
with
s\in[\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1})
such that

\frac{x-\zeta(s)}{|x-\zeta(s)|}\in\partial^{-}\zeta(s),\quad\zeta(s)+\frac{x-\zeta(s)}{|x-\zeta(s)|}\epsilon\notin\zeta([\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}))\ \text{for $\epsilon\ll 1$},

and in case there are effectively two points, the only one for which the segment
(\zeta(s),x)
is not crossing the half line
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})+\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})\mathbb{R}^{+}
is the larger one: we denote this value by the function

\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\big(S_{\ell}\cup\big\{\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})+\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})\mathbb{R}^{+}\big\}\big)\ni x\mapsto\upsilon(x).

Since the optimal ray must be in the supporting direction when touching the barrier, we deduce that the structure of the optimal ray is given by

(2.12)

\gamma_{x}=\zeta([\bar{s}_{\ell},\upsilon(x)))\cup[\zeta(\upsilon(x)),x],

where we used the convexity of
\zeta([\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}))
to deduce that if the optimal ray touches
Z
in two different points
\zeta(s_{1}),\zeta(s_{2})
, it coincides with
\zeta((s_{1},s_{2}))
in between.

Step 2.
In this step we prove that the structure of the optimal rays (
2.12
) remains unchanged for the points
\zeta(s)
,
\bar{s}_{\ell+1}\leq s\leq\bar{s}_{\ell+2}
, where the last point will be defined later.
Let
\bar{s}>\bar{s}_{\ell+1}
be the first value such that

\text{there is}\ \zeta(s^{\prime})\in\big(\zeta(\upsilon(\zeta(\bar{s}))),\zeta(\bar{s})\big),\ \text{i.e. the barrier touches the optimal ray}.

This cannot happen before
\bar{s}_{\ell+1}
by the iterative assumption, see Fig:
13(b)
. In particular, the point

\zeta(s^{\prime})=\text{arg-min}\Big\{\big|\zeta(s)-\zeta(\upsilon(\zeta(\bar{s})))\big|,\zeta(s)\in\big(\zeta(\upsilon(\zeta(\bar{s}))),\zeta(\bar{s})\big)\Big\}

satisfies
s^{\prime}>\bar{s}
(the latter being the first point), and hence by the structure of the optimal ray one has

u(\zeta(s^{\prime}))-u(\zeta(\bar{s}))=-|\zeta(\bar{s})-\zeta(s^{\prime})|<0,

contradicting the monotonicity of the function
u\circ\zeta
. Hence
u(x)
defined in (
2.11
) is the time function on
\zeta([\bar{s}_{\ell+1},\bar{s}_{\ell+2}])
because for
s\in(\bar{s}_{\ell+1},\bar{s}_{\ell+2})
the optimal rays are touching
\zeta(s)
only in the last point In particular the optimal rays are given by the construction of the previous point.

Using the iterative assumption on the structure of optimal rays (i.e. the optimal ray arriving in
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})
is the initial segment plus the spiral arc), we have proved the statement on the structure of the optimal rays: the initial segment, the spiral arc
\zeta([0,\upsilon(s)])
and the final segment
[\zeta(\upsilon(s)),\zeta(s)]
.

Step 3.
We then obtain that (
2.12
) is valid for all points
\zeta(s)
, and elementary geometric considerations give that

(2.13)

\angle\big(\zeta(s)-\zeta(\upsilon(\zeta(s))),p\big)\in\bigg(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg],\quad p\in\partial^{-}\zeta(s).

The upper bound is the monotonicity, while the lower bound is because

•

the angle is
>0
at
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})
by convexity and the iterative assumption on
S_{\ell}
,

•

the derivative of the angle
\phi
(with
\mathbf{t}(\upsilon)=e^{\imath\phi(\upsilon)}
) w.r.t.
s
satisfies the distributional bound

(2.14)

\frac{d^{2}\phi}{ds^{2}}+2\frac{d\phi}{ds}\geq 0,

obtained by comparing
\zeta
with a supporting line in the point
\zeta(s)
.

Using the local convexity about
\upsilon(x)
, again by elementary geometry one can show that

s\mapsto\phi(s)\quad\text{such that}\quad e^{\imath\phi(s)}=\frac{\zeta(s)-\zeta(\upsilon(\zeta(s)))}{|\zeta(s)-\zeta(\upsilon(\zeta(s)))|}

is locally Lipschitz, with right derivative

(2.15)

\frac{d\phi}{ds}=\frac{(\zeta(s)-\zeta(\upsilon(\zeta(s))))\wedge\mathbf{t}^{+}(s)}{|\zeta(s)-\zeta(\upsilon(\zeta(s)))|^{2}}.

Hence, if (
2.13
) holds,
\phi(s)
is locally invertible and monotone strictly increasing.

Therefore the next angle
\bar{\phi}_{\ell+2}
is given by (
2.9
) and the last point
\bar{s}_{\ell+2}
is given by (
2.10
).

Step 4.
The function
s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi)
is defined as the inverse of the function
s\mapsto\phi(s)
above, and the previous point shows that it is Lipschitz and strictly increasing, bijective with Lipschitz inverse. Here we notice that because of (
2.13
), we have that it has Lipschitz inverse in the whole interval
[\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1},\bar{\phi}_{\ell+2}]
, which would correspond to Point (2) of the statement.

The function
s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi)
is given by

(2.16)

s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi)=\upsilon(\zeta(s^{+}(\phi)))=\max\big\{s:e^{\imath\phi}\in\partial^{-}\zeta(s)\big\}.

The latter representation shows that
s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi)
is monotone increasing, right continuous, coinciding locally with the right inverse of
s\mapsto\{\phi,e^{\imath\phi}\in\partial^{-}\zeta(s)\}
, and then BV.

Finally

r_{\ell}(\phi)=\big|\zeta(s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi))-\zeta(s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi))\big|

is clearly BV, right continuous and

\begin{split}D^{\mathrm{sing}}r_{\ell}(\phi)&=-\frac{\zeta(s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi))-\zeta(s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi))}{|\zeta(s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi))-\zeta(s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi))|}\cdot D^{\mathrm{cantor}}\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))\\
&\quad+\sum_{s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi)\ \text{jumps}}\Big(\big|\zeta(s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi))-\zeta(s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi+))\big|-\big|\zeta(s^{+}_{\ell}(\phi))-\zeta(s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi-))\big|\Big)\mathfrak{d}_{\phi}.\end{split}

Formula (
2.16
) gives that the singular parts of
\zeta(s^{-}_{\ell}(\phi))
have direction
e^{\imath\phi}
and are positive, so that
D^{\mathrm{sing}}r_{\ell}\leq 0
.

Step 5.
It remains to prove the structure of the set
S_{\ell+1}
. The previous points shows that the convex curve
\zeta\llcorner_{[\bar{s}_{\ell+1},\bar{s}_{\ell+2}]}
rotates about
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})
until it crosses the half-line
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})+\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})\mathbb{R}^{+}
in the point
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+2})
: its star shaped structure follows by elementary considerations.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

We observe that the choice of the intervals
\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}=\bar{\phi}_{\ell}+2\pi+\angle(\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell}),e^{\imath\bar{\phi}_{\ell}})
can be changed into

(2.17)

\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}\in\bar{\phi}_{\ell}+2\pi+\big(\angle(\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell}),e^{\imath\bar{\phi}_{\ell}}),\angle(\mathbf{t}^{+}(\bar{s}_{\ell}),e^{\imath\bar{\phi}_{\ell}})\big)

without any variation in the proof, the idea being that in any case we know the evolution of the spiral for

\phi\in\bar{\phi}_{\ell}+2\pi+\big(\angle(\mathbf{t}^{-}(\bar{s}_{\ell}),e^{\imath\phi_{\ell}},\angle(\mathbf{t}^{+}(\bar{s}_{\ell}),e^{\imath\phi_{\ell}})\big).

More precisely, one has used in Step 1 of the previous proof the barrier

\hat{\zeta}_{\ell}(s)=\begin{cases}\zeta(s)&s\in[\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}),\\
\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1})+\mathbf{v}(s-\bar{s}_{\ell+1})&s\geq\bar{s}_{\ell+1},\end{cases}\mathbf{v}\in\partial^{-}\zeta(\bar{s}_{\ell+1}).

This observation is useful when taking limits of sequences of spirals, in which cases every round converges to a round satisfying (
2.17
).

Figure 14
.
Parametrization of a spiral-like strategy. Here the parametrization is given by the geometry of the fire rays. The angle
\phi^{\prime}
is obtained by the angle
\phi
through Equation (
3.1
), while
r(\phi^{\prime})
represents the length of the last segment of the fire ray.

By piecing together the function
s^{-}_{\ell},s^{+}_{\ell},r_{\ell}
of the previous proposition we obtain the following

Theorem 2.13
.

There exist
0\leq\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{s1bar}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{s}_{1}}}<\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{s2bar}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{s}_{2}}},\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{sbar}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{s}}}<S
,
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{phibar}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{\phi}}}\geq 2\pi
and
3
functions

[2\pi,\bar{\phi}]\ni\phi\mapsto\big(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{rphi}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r(\phi)}},\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{s-phi}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s^{-}(\phi)}},\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{s+phi}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s^{+}(\phi)}}\big)\in[0,+\infty)\times[\bar{s}_{1},\bar{s}]\times[\bar{s}_{2},S]

such that the following hold:

(1)

r(\phi)
is one-sided Lipschitz and right continuous, positive for all
\phi<\bar{\phi}
and the spiral
\zeta
is closed iff
r(\bar{\phi})=0
;

(2)

s^{-}(\phi)
is monotone increasing, right continuous, and
s^{-}(2\pi)=\bar{s}_{1}
,
s^{-}(\bar{\phi})=\bar{s}
;

(3)

s^{+}(\phi)
is uniformly Lipschitz, bijective with Lipschitz inverse and increasing, and
s^{+}(2\pi)=s_{2}
,
s^{+}(\bar{\phi})=S
;

(4)

it holds

e^{\imath\phi}\in\partial^{-}\zeta(s^{-}(\phi)),\quad\zeta(s^{+}(\phi))=\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+r(\phi)e^{\imath\phi};

(5)

if
s^{+}(\phi)=s^{-}(\phi^{\prime})
, then

\phi^{\prime}-\phi-2\pi\in\bigg(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg].

The structure of optimal rays given in the previous theorem motivates the following

Definition 2.14
.

The point
\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))
will be called
base point
of the optimal ray arriving in
\zeta(s^{+}(\phi))
.

We define the two following functions:

(2.18)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{beta-}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\beta^{-}(\phi)}}=\angle\big(\mathbf{t}^{-}(s^{+}(\phi)),e^{\imath\phi}\big),\quad\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{beta+}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\beta^{+}(\phi)}}=\angle\big(\mathbf{t}^{+}(s^{+}(\phi)),e^{\imath\phi}\big).

Being
s^{+}(\phi)
Lipschitz and
\mathbf{t}(s)
a BV function, it follows that
\beta^{\pm}(\phi)
are the right/left representative of the BV function

(2.19)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{beta}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\beta(\phi)}}=\angle\big(\dot{\zeta}(s^{+}(\phi)),e^{\imath\phi}\big).

The range of these functions is
(0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
in the interval
\phi>0
by (
2.13
), and clearly
D^{\mathrm{sing}}\beta\geq 0
, using the same estimate as (
2.14
): more precisely we have the one-sided Lipschitz estimate

(2.20)

D\beta+\mathscr{L}^{1}\geq 0,

obtained again by comparison with a supporting line at
\zeta(s^{+}(\phi))
. We will equivalently use the notation

\beta^{\pm}(s)=\beta^{\pm}(\phi)\quad\text{where}\quad s=s^{+}(\phi).

Define the
instantaneous burning rate
as

(2.21)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{b(t)}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b(t)}}=D_{t}\mathscr{H}^{1}(Z\cap\overline{R^{Z}(t)})=D_{t}(u\circ\zeta)^{-1}([0,t]).

The following proposition provides a formula for the admissibility functional (
2.4
) in the case of spiral strategies, due to the previous geometric properties of these barriers. Since it is a fairly easy consequence of the previous results, we will not provide a proof.

Proposition 2.15
.

The admissibility functional (see Equation (
2.4
))
\mathcal{A}
as a function of
\phi
can be written as

\mathcal{A}(\phi)=1+s^{-}(\phi)+r(\phi)-\frac{1}{\sigma}s^{+}(\phi),

and the right continuous representative of the a.c. part of the burning rate function
b(s)
is

(2.22)

b^{+}(t)=\frac{1}{\cos(\beta^{+}(s))},\quad u(\zeta(s))=t.

As a simple application, we have:

Corollary 2.16
.

If
\zeta(s)
is saturated, then
\beta^{+}(s)\leq\arccos(\frac{1}{\sigma})
.

Proof.

By (
2.22
) we must have for saturated points
b^{+}\leq\sigma
, which is the statement.
∎

3.
RDE description of a spiral

In this section we will give a representation of a spiral strategy
Z
by means of a Retarded Differential Equation (RDE), based on the angle-ray representation of Theorem
2.13
. This is a generalization of the RDE obtained in
[
Kle19
]
, where the angle
\beta(\phi)
is equal to
\alpha=\arccos(\frac{1}{\sigma})
. If one considers
\beta(\phi)
as a control parameter, the spiral is described by the linear RDE (
3.3
) with control
\beta
, which in the case
\beta(\phi)
is continuous becomes

\frac{d}{d\phi}r(\phi^{\prime})=\cot(\beta(\phi^{\prime})r(\phi^{\prime})-\frac{r(\phi)}{\sin(\beta(\phi))},\quad\phi^{\prime}=\phi+2\pi+\beta(\phi).

The term
\frac{r(\phi)}{\sin(\beta(\phi))}
is exactly the curvature of the spiral at the base point of
r(\phi^{\prime})
(see Definition
2.14
). Observe moreover that the delay interval

\phi^{\prime}-\phi=2\pi+\beta(\phi)

depends on the control parameter at the previous round, unless
\beta
is constant.

The main result of this section is the equivalence between the solution to the RDE above and convex spiral barriers (Proposition
3.5
) and the analysis of the solution for
\beta(\phi)=\alpha
constant: in particular the key criterion (Lemma
3.14
) to check if the solution
r(\phi)
is diverging and its application to the saturated spiral (Proposition
3.16
). In addition, we compute some Green kernels for the linear RDE and study some of their properties: they will be useful in the next chapters.

We start now by writing the RDE for spiral barriers.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
Z
be a spiral (Definition
2.10
) and let
\phi\rightarrow\beta(\phi)
(alternatively
s\rightarrow\beta(s)
) as in (
2.19
). Then the following formulas hold:

(1)

The relation
s^{-}(\phi^{\prime})=s^{+}(\phi)
has solutions (see Figures
14
)

(3.1)

\phi^{\prime}\in\phi+2\pi+[\beta^{-}(\phi),\beta^{+}(\phi)].

(2)

It holds

(3.2)

\frac{d}{d\phi}s^{+}(\phi)=\frac{r(\phi)}{\sin(\beta(\phi))}.

(3)

If
\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))
is a differentiability points of
Z
with
\ddot{\zeta}(s^{-}(\phi))\not=0
, the curvature

\kappa
is given by

\kappa=\frac{1}{R}=\frac{d\phi}{ds^{-}(\phi)},

and, for
\phi>2\pi
, the spiral satisfies the equation

(3.3)

\frac{d}{d\phi}r(\phi)=\cot(\beta(\phi))r(\phi)-\frac{d}{d\phi}s^{-}(\phi)

in the sense of distributions.

Since the functions involved are BV by Theorem
2.13
, we will use also the notation

Dr=\cot(\beta)r\mathscr{L}^{1}+Ds^{-}

in the sense of measures.

The next definition corresponds to a generalization of the linkage introduced in
[
Kle19
]
.

Definition 3.2

(Subsequent angles)
.

Given the rotation angle
\phi
, we will refer to the angles
\phi^{\prime}
given by (
3.1
) as
subsequent angles
.

Remark 3.3
.

In the case of the first round the equation for spirals reads as follows

(3.4)

\frac{dr}{ds}=\cos(\beta(s)),

or, in the variable
\phi
,

\frac{d}{d\phi}r(\phi)=\cot(\beta(\phi))r(\phi).

Note that (
3.3
) holds for all
\phi
by extending
s^{-}([0,2\pi))=0
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

The first point is a consequence of Points (4),(5) of Theorem
2.13
and the definition of
\beta^{\pm}
in (
2.18
) (see Figure
14
).

Point (
2
) is the inverse of (
2.15
).

Finally Point (
3
) follows by projecting the derivative of

\zeta(s^{+}(\phi))=\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+e^{\imath\phi}r(\phi)

on the direction
e^{\imath\phi}
and using Point (
2
).
∎

Figure 15
.
In a point of non-differentiability
P
the variation of the radius is computed as
\frac{d}{d\phi}r(\phi)=\cot\beta(\phi)r(\phi)
. The opposite situation occurs at the poin
R
, where the
r
has a downward jump of size equal to the length of the segment
[Q,R]
.

Remark 3.4
.

In the case of
\frac{ds^{-}}{d\phi}=R(\phi)\mathscr{L}^{1}
,
R(\phi)
being the curvature of
\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))
, we obtain the RDE

(3.5)

\frac{d}{d\phi}r(\phi)=\cot\beta(\phi)r(\phi)-R(\phi).

When
s^{-}
jumps (or it has a singular part), then
r(\phi)
has the same singular part with opposite sign, in particular it jumps down, as observed in the proof of Proposition
2.11
.

If instead
s^{-}
is constant, i.e. there is a corner point in
\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))
, then the ODE becomes

\frac{dr(\phi)}{d\phi}=\cot\beta(\phi)r(\phi).

See Figure
15
.

In the next proposition we show that the one-sided Lipschitz bound on
\beta(\phi)
given by (
2.20
) suffices to construct the spiral
\zeta
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Assume that the function
\beta:(0,\infty)\to(0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
satisfies

(3.6)

D\beta+\mathscr{L}^{1}\geq 0,\quad\text{and}\quad\beta((0,\epsilon))>0,\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\frac{d\phi}{\beta(\phi)}<\infty\ \text{for some }\ \epsilon\ll 1.

Then, there exists an angle
\bar{\phi}\in(0,+\infty]
depending only on
\beta
with the following properties: for every given
r(0)
, there is a unique spiral
\zeta
such that the functions
r(\phi),s^{-}(\phi),s^{+}(\phi)
,
\phi\in[0,\bar{\phi})
constructed in Theorem
2.11
satisfy the equations

(3.7)

\zeta(s^{+}(\phi))=\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+r(\phi)e^{\imath\phi},\quad\frac{dr}{d\phi}=\cot(\beta)r+\frac{ds^{-}}{d\phi},\quad r(0)=\lim_{\phi\searrow 0}r(\phi).

If
\bar{\phi}<+\infty
, then as
\phi\nearrow\bar{\phi}
either
r(\phi)\searrow 0
or
r(\phi)\nearrow\infty
with asymptotic direction
e^{\imath\bar{\phi}}
.

The integrability of
\beta^{-1}
near
\phi=0
is needed to construct an initial arc of the spiral, since the one-sided Lipschitz assumptions on
\beta
will take care of the integrability at the next angles. The positivity assumption is just the requirement that we are rotating counterclockwise. We remark that every
\beta
which is locally 1-sided Lipschitz in
(0,\epsilon)
can be extended to a 1-sided Lipschitz function on
(0,\infty)
, so that
\beta
defined on
(0,+\infty)
is not a requirement.

Proof.

We start with the first round, where the angle-ray parametrization corresponds to the polar coordinates: the ODE is then (
3.4
), whose solution is

(3.8)

r(\phi)=r(0)e^{\int_{0}^{\phi}\cot(\beta)\mathscr{L}^{1}}.

Due to the assumption that
\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\frac{d\phi}{\beta(\phi)}<\infty
, the above formula is locally meaningful.

The curve is given by

\zeta(\phi)=r(\phi)e^{\imath\phi},

and it is a fairly easy computation to show that (
3.6
) implies the convexity of
\zeta
. Note that if
\beta
converges to
0
for
\phi\nearrow\bar{\phi}
, then by the 1-sided Lipschitz condition (
3.6
) we get
\beta(\phi)\leq(\bar{\phi}-\phi)
, so that
r(\phi)
given by (
3.8
) diverges as
\phi\to\bar{\phi}
with asymptotic direction
e^{\imath\bar{\phi}}
, and in this case the construction procedure stops. Note that for this case
r(\phi)\geq r(0)
, so that if
r
is not blowing up the polar representation stops when
\phi=2\pi
. For coherence with the notation of Proposition
2.11
, we will set

\bar{s}_{1}=0,\quad\bar{\phi}_{1}=2\pi,\quad\bar{s}_{2}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{r(\phi)}{\sin(\beta(\phi))}d\phi,

the latter being the length of the spiral in the first round.

Assume that the spiral has been constructed up to the rotation angle
\bar{\phi}_{\ell}
, with length
\bar{s}_{\ell+1}
: more precisely,

•

the curve
\zeta(\phi)
,
\phi\in[\bar{\phi}_{\ell-1},\bar{\phi}_{\ell}]
, is locally convex, and it can be represented as in Proposition
2.11
: we will write
\zeta(s)
,
s\in[\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}]
, for its length parametrization, and we will use the notation of convex spirals;

•

\zeta(\bar{\phi}_{\ell})\in\zeta(\bar{\phi}_{\ell-1})+\mathbf{t}^{-}(\phi_{\ell-1})\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

In particular, by defining the next angle

e^{\imath\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}}=\mathbf{t}^{-}(\phi_{\ell}),\quad\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}\in\bar{\phi}_{\ell}+2\pi+\bigg(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg],

the function

[\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1})\ni\phi\mapsto s^{-}(\phi)\in[\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}]

is defined as in (
2.16
): it is the right continuous inverse of the monotone function
[\bar{s}_{\ell},\bar{s}_{\ell+1}]\ni s\mapsto\mathbf{t}(s)=e^{\imath\phi}
,
\phi\in[\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}]
. We can also apply Step 1 of the proof of Proposition
2.11
to construct the time function
u
for the next round, see (
2.11
).

Given the function
\beta(\phi)
,
\phi\in[\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}]
, we now solve the ODE (
3.3
), namely

Dr=\cot(\beta)r\mathscr{L}^{1}-Ds^{-},

yielding a unique right continuous BV function
r(\phi)
,
\phi\in[\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}]
. The domain of
r(\phi)
will be the set
[\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{\phi})=\big\{r(\phi)\in(0,\infty)\big\}
.

We can prolong the spiral
\zeta
for
\phi\in[\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{\phi})
by defining the curve

\zeta(\phi)=\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+r(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}.

We need to show that this is actually a curve and it is Lipschitz, locally convex, and
\phi\mapsto u(\zeta(\phi))
is monotone, where
u
is well defined as in the proof of Proposition
2.11
, see Formula (
2.12
) for the explicit computation of the time function at every round.

Taking the derivative and observing that
D\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))=e^{\imath\phi}|D\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|
by definition, we have

D\zeta=e^{\imath\phi}\big(Ds^{-}+Dr\big)+\imath re^{\imath\phi}=(\cot(\beta)+\imath)re^{\imath\phi},

where we recall that
\imath
is the imaginary unit. This shows that
\zeta
is Lipschitz whenever
r<\infty
, and moreover that

\frac{d}{d\phi}u\circ\zeta=\cot(\beta)r(\phi)>0,

giving the monotonicity.

Being
\phi\mapsto\beta(\phi)
a BV function by (
3.6
), we can further differentiate
D\zeta
obtaining

\begin{split}D\frac{D\zeta}{|D\zeta|}&=D[(\cos(\beta)+\imath\sin(\beta))e^{\imath\phi}]=(D\cos(\beta)+\imath D\sin(\beta))e^{\imath\phi}+(\imath\cos(\beta)-\sin(\beta))e^{\imath\phi}.\end{split}

Taking the scalar product with

\frac{D\zeta^{\perp}}{|D\zeta|}=\bigg(-\sin\bigg(\frac{\beta^{+}(\phi)+\beta^{-}(\phi)}{2}\bigg)+\imath\cos\bigg(\frac{\beta^{+}(\phi)+\beta^{-}(\phi)}{2}\bigg)\bigg)e^{\imath\phi},

we get

\begin{split}\frac{D\zeta^{\perp}}{|D\zeta|}\cdot D\frac{D\zeta}{|D\zeta|}&=1+\cos\bigg(\frac{\beta^{+}+\beta^{-}}{2}\bigg)D\sin(\beta)-\sin\bigg(\frac{\beta^{+}+\beta^{-}}{2}\bigg)D\cos(\beta)\\
&=\frac{\sin(\frac{\beta^{+}-\beta^{-}}{2})}{\frac{\beta^{+}-\beta^{-}}{2}}D^{\mathrm{jump}}\beta+\mathscr{L}^{1}+D^{\mathrm{cont}}\beta.\end{split}

Hence (
3.6
) implies
\zeta
is convex. We thus conclude that it is a spiral according to Definition
2.10
, once we reparametrized it by length.

If
\beta(\phi)\searrow 0
as
\phi\nearrow\bar{\phi}
, the same conclusion as for the first round applies, i.e. the spirals blow up with asymptotic direction
e^{\imath\check{\phi}}
. If
r(\phi)\searrow 0
, then the curve
\zeta(\phi)
closes at the angle
\bar{\phi}
. Otherwise, we can prolong the spiral to the next round
\phi\in[\bar{\phi}_{\ell},\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}]
.

It is clear that such a construction gives a unique solution
r(\phi)
and curve
\zeta(\phi)
, and by construction at each round the angle-ray representation coincides with the one of Proposition
2.11
.

The last steps in the proof is to prove that
\bar{\phi}
does not depend on
r(0)
: for this, we just observe that the equations (
3.7
) are linear w.r.t.
r,\zeta
, so that
r(\phi)=r(0)r_{r(0)=1}(\phi)
and similarly
\zeta(\phi)=r(0)\zeta_{r(0)=1}(\phi)
. ∎

We conclude this section by observing that in case of constant angle
\beta(\phi)\equiv\beta
, the radius of curvature is

R(\phi^{\prime})=\frac{r(\phi)}{\sin(\beta)},

and then the equation (
3.5
) reads as

\frac{dr}{d\phi^{\prime}}(\phi^{\prime})=\cot(\beta)r(\phi^{\prime})-\frac{r(\phi)}{\sin(\beta)},\quad\phi^{\prime}=2\pi+\phi+\beta.

This is a linear RDE with constant coefficients introduced first by
[
Kle19
]
, written as

(3.9)

\frac{d}{d\phi}r(\phi)=\cot(\beta)r(\phi)-\frac{r(\phi-(2\pi+\beta))}{\sin(\beta)},

which we are going to study in the next section.

3.1.
Analysis of the Saturated Spiral

We start with the definition of saturated spiral, with the initial set of the fire
R_{0}=B_{1}(0)
: even if it is not the initial fire set we consider, it will serve as a simpler situation where we develop one of the building blocks of our analysis. The analysis for the ”saturated spiral” when
R_{0}=B_{r}(0)
,
r\in(0,1)
, or
R_{0}=\{(0,0)\}
will be done in Section
5
. In particular, it is a simple observation that given an admissible spiral
\bar{\zeta}
up to the angle
\phi_{0}
where
\zeta(\phi_{0})
is saturated, continuing
\bar{\zeta}
as a saturated spiral (i.e. solving (
3.3
) with
\beta=\arccos(\frac{1}{\sigma})
constant for
r(\phi)
is the angle-ray coordinates) gives an admissible spiral up to the angle where
r(\phi)
may become
0
: indeed by definition the rate of construction is equal to
\sigma
.

Definition 3.6
.

We define the
saturated spiral
as the admissible spiral
Z_{\mathrm{sat}}
with the following property:

\mathcal{S}(Z_{\mathrm{sat}})=\big\{t\in[0,T]:\mathscr{H}^{1}(Z_{\mathrm{sat}}\cap\overline{R^{Z_{\mathrm{sat}}}(t)})=\sigma t\big\}=[0,T].

Clearly the above definition implies that
\Omega_{0}=B_{1}(0)
, otherwise
(1,0)\in Z_{\mathrm{sat}}
is not saturated.

Saturated spirals are strategies built assuming the instantaneous speed of construction is constant and takes the maximum value
\sigma
, that is the burning rate function
b(t)
is constantly equal to
\sigma
.

The formula for burning rates (
2.22
) tells us that, in case of saturated spirals, the angle between the fire ray and the spiral is constantly equal to

\beta^{+}(\phi)=\beta^{-}(\phi)=\arccos\bigg(\frac{1}{\sigma}\bigg),

which will be denoted by
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{alphaspeed}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\alpha}}=\arccos(\frac{1}{\sigma})
.

The first lemma addresses the first round: it is just the integration of the linear ODE (
3.4
), which is now with constant coefficient
\cot(\beta(\phi))=\cot(\alpha)
, and it has been obtained already in
[
Kle19
]
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
Z_{\mathrm{sat}}
be a saturated spiral. Then, in the angle-ray representation
\zeta(\phi)=\zeta(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})+r_{\mathrm{sat}}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}
, the ray
r

sat

(ϕ)
satisfies the RDE

(3.10)

\frac{d}{d\phi}r_{\mathrm{sat}}(\phi)=r_{\mathrm{sat}}(\phi)\cot(\alpha)-\frac{r_{\mathrm{sat}}(\phi-(2\pi+\alpha))}{\sin(\alpha)},

with initial data

r_{\mathrm{sat},0}(\phi)=\begin{cases}e^{\cot(\alpha)\phi}&\forall\phi\in[0,2\pi),\\
(e^{2\pi\cot(\alpha)}-1)e^{\cot(\alpha)(\phi-2\pi)}&\forall\phi\in[2\pi,2\pi+\alpha].\end{cases}

Proof.

For
\phi\in[0,2\pi]
it is a consequence of the RDE which reduces to the ODE
\dot{r}=\cot(\alpha)r
. Assume
\phi\in[2\pi,2\pi+\alpha]
, one easily computes that, calling
P=(1,0)
, the saturated spiral is a logarithmic spiral centered at
P
with initial radius
r(2\pi)-1
. Finally, (
3.10
) follows by (
3.9
) for which the angle of the spiral is constantly equal to
\alpha
.
∎

In the next section we study the RDE of the saturated spiral.

3.2.
Formulation as a retarded ODE

In this subsection we study in detail the RDE (
3.10
), and we find for which angle
\alpha
(or equivalently for which burning rate) the associated spiral confines the fire. Part of these results have been already obtained in
[
Kle19
]
(we repeat them for completeness using a different approach based on RDE analysis), with also a new direct proof that the saturated spiral constructed with speed
\sigma\leq\bar{\sigma}=2.6144..
does not confine the fire (Proposition
3.16
): the speed
\bar{\sigma}
is called
critical speed
, and it is computed as the solution to an eigen-equation.

The proof of this result exploits a change of variable in the equation (
3.10
), which will be of key importance also in the next sections (Lemma
3.14
). we refer mainly to
[
Hal93
]
for the theory of RDEs.

3.2.1.
Change of variables

Define the constant

(3.11)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{cexp}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}=\frac{\ln(\frac{2\pi+\alpha}{\sin(\alpha)})}{2\pi+\alpha},

and rescale the solution
r(\phi)
to (
3.10
) as

(3.12)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{rhogrowt}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\rho(\tau)}}=r((2\pi+\alpha)\tau)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau}.

By direct computations

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}

\displaystyle=(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau}\bigg(\frac{d}{d\phi}r((2\pi+\alpha)\tau)-cr(2\pi+\alpha)\bigg)

\displaystyle=(2\pi+\alpha)(\cot(\alpha)-c)\rho(\tau)-\frac{(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)}}{\sin(\alpha)}\rho(\tau-1)

\displaystyle=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{acost}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a(\alpha)}}\rho(\tau)-\rho(\tau-1),

with

(3.13)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{acost}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a(\alpha)}}=(2\pi+\alpha)(\cot(\alpha)-c(\alpha))=(2\pi+\alpha)\cot(\alpha)-\ln\bigg(\frac{2\pi+\alpha}{\sin(\alpha)}\bigg).

We will now study the spectrum of the RDE

(3.14)

\dot{\rho}(\tau)=a\rho(\tau)-\rho(\tau-1).

The characteristic equation for the eigenvalues
\lambda
is

(3.15)

\lambda+e^{-\lambda}-a=0.

We start by studying the real eigenvalues: the following result has been obtained also by
[
Bre08
,
Kle19
]
.

Lemma 3.8

(Real Eigenvalues)
.

The following hold.

(1)

If
a>1
, there are two real eigenvalues
\lambda_{0}^{-},\lambda_{0}^{+}
with
\lambda_{0}^{-}<0<\lambda_{0}^{+}
with algebraic multiplicity
1
.

(2)

If
a=1
, there exists a unique real eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=0
with algebraic multiplicity
2
.

(3)

If
a<1
there are no real eigenvalues.

Proof.

It is elementary to see that the function
\lambda\mapsto\lambda+e^{-\lambda}
has a global minimum at
\lambda=0
with value
1
and it is strictly convex.
∎

From the point of view of solutions we thus have
[
Hal93
, Theorem 4.1]
the following

Corollary 3.9
.

The following holds:

(1)

If
a>1
, there are two solutions to (
3.14
) of the form

\rho^{-}(\tau)=e^{\lambda_{0}^{-}\tau},\quad\rho^{+}(\tau)=e^{\lambda_{0}^{+}\tau},

the first exponentially decreasing while the second exponentially increasing. The two
real eigenvalues
\lambda_{0}^{-},\lambda_{0}^{+}
with
\lambda_{0}^{-}<0<\lambda_{0}^{+}
have algebraic multiplicity
1
.

(2)

If
a=1
, there exists two solutions of the form

\rho(\tau)=1,\quad\rho(\tau)=\tau.

Since the function equation

\alpha\mapsto a(\alpha)=(2\pi+\alpha)\bigg(\cot(\alpha)+\frac{\ln(\frac{2\pi+\alpha}{\sin(\alpha)})}{2\pi+\alpha}\bigg)

has derivative

-\frac{2\pi+\alpha}{\sin^{2}(\alpha)}+(2\pi+\alpha)\cot(\alpha)=\frac{2\pi+\alpha}{\sin^{2}(\alpha)}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sin(2\alpha)-1\bigg)<0,

by inverting it we obtain the following (see
[
Kle19
, Lemma 6]
):

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
\bar{\alpha}
be such that

(2\pi+\alpha)\bigg(\cot(\alpha)+\frac{\ln(\frac{2\pi+\alpha}{\sin(\alpha)})}{2\pi+\alpha}\bigg)=1,\quad\bar{\alpha}=1.1783..,

and

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{barsigma}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{\sigma}}}=\frac{1}{\cos(\bar{\alpha})}=2.61443....

Then the following hold.

•

If
\alpha<\bar{\alpha}
(
\sigma>\bar{\sigma})
, there are two exponential solutions to (
3.10
)

r^{-}(\phi)=e^{(c(\alpha)+\frac{\lambda^{-}_{0}}{2\pi+\alpha})\phi},\quad r^{-}(\phi)=e^{(c(\alpha)+\frac{\lambda^{+}_{0}}{2\pi+\alpha})\phi}

with

c(\alpha)+\frac{\lambda^{-}_{0}}{2\pi+\alpha}<\bar{c}=\cot(\bar{\alpha})-\frac{1}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}<c(\alpha)+\frac{\lambda^{+}_{0}}{2\pi+\alpha}.

•

If
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}
(
\sigma=\bar{\sigma}
), there exist two solutions to (
3.10
)

r(\phi)=e^{\bar{c}\phi},\quad r(\phi)=\phi e^{\bar{c}\phi},

with

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{cbar}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{c}}}=\cot(\bar{\alpha})-\frac{1}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}=0.27995...

•

If
\alpha>\bar{\alpha}
(
\sigma>\bar{\sigma}
), there are no purely exponential solutions to (
3.10
), i.e. every solution oscillates.

We now turn the attention to the complex eigenvalues
\lambda=x+\imath y
:

x+\imath y+e^{-x}\big(\cos(y)-\imath\sin(y)\big)=a,

which gives

(3.16)

x=\ln\bigg(\frac{\sin(y)}{y}\bigg),\quad|y|\in(-\pi,\pi)+2k\pi,

and

(3.17)

f(y)=\frac{e^{y\cot y}\sin y}{y}=e^{a}>0.

The function
y\mapsto f(y)=e^{y\cot y}\frac{\sin y}{y}
has the properties:

Figure 16
.
Plot of the function
f(y)
, Equation (
3.17
).

•

it is symmetric, so we need to study only the case
y>0
(positive real part), and
f(0)=e
(which is the critical case for the real eigenvalues);

•

it is positive when
\sin(y)>0
, i.e. for

y\in(0,\pi)+2k\pi,\quad k=0,1,\dots,

negative in

y\in(\pi,2\pi)+2k\pi,\quad k=0,1,\dots,

and

\lim_{y\to k\pi-}f(y)=0,\quad\lim_{y\to k\pi+}f(y)=\begin{cases}-\infty&k\ \text{odd},\\
+\infty&k\ \text{even};\end{cases}

moreover it is strictly monotone in each interval.

•

For each
k=1,2,\dots
, let
y_{k}
be the unique solution to

\frac{e^{y\cot y}\sin y}{y}=e^{a},\quad y\in(2k\pi,(2k+1)\pi].

Writing
y_{k}=2k\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}-\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{deltakappa}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{k}}}
, we obtain the estimate

e^{a}=\frac{e^{(2k+\frac{1}{2})\pi\delta_{k}}\cos(\delta_{k})}{(2k+\frac{\pi}{2})-\delta_{k}}\sim\frac{e^{(2k+\frac{1}{2})\pi\delta_{k}}}{(2k+\frac{\pi}{2})},

which shows that
\delta_{k}\sim\frac{\ln(k)}{2\pi k}
as
k\to\infty
.

•

The corresponding eigenvalues are

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{lambdak}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lambda_{k}}}=\ln\bigg(\frac{\sin(y_{k})}{y_{k}}\bigg)+\imath y_{k}=a-\frac{y_{k}}{\sin(y_{k})}\big(\cos(y_{k})-\imath\sin(y_{k})\big),\quad|\lambda_{k}-a|=\frac{y_{k}}{\sin(y_{k})}.

By means of the asymptotic estimate of
y_{k}
we get

\lambda_{k}-a=-\frac{(2k+\frac{1}{2})\pi}{\cos(\delta_{k})}(\sin(\delta_{k})-\imath\cos(\delta_{k}))\simeq-\ln(k)+\imath k.

Hence they asymptotically lie on the curve

x\simeq-\ln(y).

•

Since every
0
of (
3.17
) is simple, the complex eigenvalues are simple.

Reconstructing the eigenexponent for the original RDE (
1.7
), we have proved the following lemma (see
[
Kle19
, Lemma 6]
).

Lemma 3.11

(Complex eigenvalues)
.

The following hold.

(1)

There is a sequence of complex conjugate exponents indexed by
k=1,2,\dots

(3.18)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{omegak}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\omega_{k}}}=c(\alpha)+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2\pi+\alpha}=\cot(\alpha)-\frac{y_{k}}{2\pi+\alpha}(\cot(y_{k})\pm\imath),\quad y_{k}=2k\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}-\delta_{k},\ \delta_{k}\simeq\frac{\ln(k)}{2k\pi},

such that the corresponding eigensolution to (
3.10
) is

r_{k}(\phi)=e^{\omega^{\pm}_{k}\phi};

(2)

if
\alpha>\bar{\alpha}
, there is a couple of complex conjugate exponents
\omega_{0}
computed according to (
3.18
) with
y_{0}\in(0,\pi)
;

(3)

for
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}
the couple at
|y|<\pi
of the previous point merges to the exponent

\bar{c}=\cot(\bar{\alpha})-\frac{1}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}.

3.2.2.
Application to saturated spiral strategies

Aim of this section is to prove that the saturated spiral confines the fire for
\sigma>\bar{\sigma}
and does not confine the fire for
\sigma\leq\bar{\sigma}
. The first statement is quite easy and it has been already proved in
[
Bre08
]
and
[
Kle19
, Theorem 1]
, while the second is a consequence of the estimate on the number
n
of rounds needed to close the saturated spiral, i.e.
n\sim\Im(\omega_{0})^{-1}
given in
[
Kle19
, Theorem 4]
. In this section we present a different approach for this second result which can be adapted to general spirals, and obtain the asymptotic behavior.

We assume first that
\sigma>\bar{\sigma}
and consider as initial data for the RDE (
1.7
) any admissible spiral. This is the case where a firefighter constructs any admissible spiral with construction speed
\sigma
up to some point
Q
, with the property that in the point
Q
it holds
\beta^{-}(Q)\leq\bar{\alpha}
, and then it starts constructing a spiral with constant angle
\bar{\alpha}
. We will call
saturated branches
the arcwise connected subsets of spiral-like strategies constructed with constant angle
\bar{\alpha}
.

The new spiraling strategy obtained in this way is still convex and it is admissible, being the point
Q
admissible: indeed, if
Z
is this particular spiral strategy, then

\mathscr{H}^{1}(S\cap\overline{R^{Z}(t)})=\mathscr{H}^{1}(Z\cap\overline{R^{Z}(s_{Q})})+\sigma(t-s_{Q})\leq\sigma t,\quad\forall t\geq s_{Q},

with
s_{Q}=u(Q)
(
u
is the minimum time function), where we have used that the burning rate (
2.21
) is constantly equal to
\sigma
and the fact that
Q
is admissible.

The previous discussion on the complex spectrum of the operator justifies the following statement.

Proposition 3.12

(
[
Bre08
,
Kle19
]
)
.

If
\sigma>\bar{\sigma}
, then for any initial data the saturated spiral confines the fire.

Proof.

We consider the change of variables

\rho(\tau)=r((2\pi+\alpha)\tau)e^{-(2\pi+\alpha)\cot(\alpha)\tau},

then the function
\rho
satisfies the following RDE:

\dot{\rho}(\tau)+\frac{(2\pi+\alpha)}{\sin(\alpha)}e^{-(2\pi+\alpha)\cot(\alpha)}\rho(\tau-1)=0.

It is a well known fact that every solution of a RDE of the form
\dot{f}(\tau)+cf(\tau-r)=0
with
c>0
is oscillating iff the characteristic equation
\lambda+ce^{-\lambda r}=0
has only complex eigenvalues (see for example
[
Mys51
]
).
∎

Remark 3.13
.

Observing that by (
3.18
)

\Re(\omega_{k})\leq\Re(\omega_{1})<\cot(\alpha)-\frac{2\pi}{2\pi+\alpha}\leq\cot(\bar{\alpha})-\frac{2\pi}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}=-0.428111,

so that for
\alpha\searrow\bar{\alpha}
the only surviving eigenvectors are the ones corresponding to
\omega_{0}
, which takes about
\frac{\pi}{y_{0}}
to close. A more precise argument using the projection of the initial data gives the estimate of
[
Kle19
, Theorem 4]
.

For the analysis of the second case, we consider (
3.14
) and use the following simple lemma.

Lemma 3.14
.

The following holds:

(1)

If
a>1
and
\rho(1)\geq\rho(\tau)>0
for every
\tau\in[0,1]
, then
\rho(\tau)
diverges exponentially.

(2)

If
a=1
and
\rho(1)>\rho(\tau)>0
,
\tau\in[0,1)
, then the solution
\rho(\tau)
diverges linearly.

(3)

If
a<1
, the solution
\rho(\tau)
oscillates.

In particular, the solution
r(\phi)
is diverging when
\rho(\tau)
satisfies the first two conditions.

Proof.

It is immediate to see that in the case
a>1
the solution
\rho(\tau)
is non decreasing for
\tau\geq 2
. Similarly when
a=1
the solution
\rho(\tau)
is strictly increasing for
\tau>2
under the assumptions of the statement. Moreover it is fairly easy to see from (
3.16
) that all complex eigenvalues have negative real part.

Thus if
a>1
the unique non decreasing eigensolution is
e^{\lambda_{0}^{+}\tau}
, which is the asymptotic limit of
\rho(\tau)
. The same for
a=1
, where the unique increasing solution is
\tau
.

The third point is due to the fact that there are only complex eigenvalues.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

Observe that the first two cases clearly hold also if

\dot{\rho}(\tau)\geq a\rho(\tau)-\rho(\tau-1),\quad a\geq 1.

We now show how this lemma can be applied to a concrete case, namely the saturated spiral
r_{\mathrm{sat}}(\phi)
of Lemma
3.7
, to prove that the strategy cannot confine the fire.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
Z
be the saturated spiral. Then it does not confine the fire for
\sigma\leq\bar{\sigma}
.

Figure 17
.
Plot of the function (
3.19
).

Proof.

Recall the initial data for the saturated spiral is given in Lemma
3.7
: it is precisely

r_{\mathrm{sat}}(\phi)=\begin{cases}e^{\cot(\alpha)\phi}&\phi\in[0,2\pi),\\
(e^{2\pi\cot(\alpha)}-1)e^{\cot(\alpha)(\phi-2\pi)}&\phi\in[2\pi,2\pi+\alpha].\end{cases}

According to Lemma
3.14
, we have to prove that for all
\phi\in[0,2\pi)

r_{\mathrm{sat}}(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)}>r_{\mathrm{sat}}(\phi)e^{-c\phi}=\begin{cases}e^{a(\alpha)\frac{\phi}{2\pi+\alpha}}&\phi\in[0,2\pi),\\
(e^{2\pi\cot(\alpha)}-1)e^{-2\pi\cot(\alpha)+a(\alpha)\frac{\phi}{2\pi+\alpha}}&\phi\in[2\pi,2\pi+\alpha);\end{cases}

it is clear that the assumption of the lemma is verified for
\phi\in[2\pi,2\pi+\bar{\alpha})
, being
r_{\mathrm{sat}}(\phi)e^{a(\alpha)\frac{\phi}{2\pi+\alpha}}
increasing in that interval since
a(\alpha)\geq 1
. It is also clear from the formula that we need only to check that

\displaystyle(e^{2\pi\cot(\alpha)}-1)e^{-2\pi\cot(\alpha)+a(\alpha)}-e^{a(\alpha)\frac{2\pi}{2\pi+\alpha}}>0.

Using (
3.13
) one can numerically verify that the function (see Fig.
17
)

(3.19)

\alpha\mapsto(e^{2\pi\cot(\alpha)}-1)e^{-2\pi\cot(\alpha)+a(\alpha)}-e^{a(\alpha)\frac{2\pi}{2\pi+\alpha}}

is decreasing w.r.t.
\alpha
, and it is positive for
\alpha<1.2
. Being the critical angle
\bar{\alpha}=1.17..<1.2
, then we can apply Lemma
3.14
and deduce that the saturated spirals does not confine the fire.
∎

3.2.3.
Green kernels of the RDE

We write now explicitly the kernel for the RDE (
3.14
), i.e. a functions
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\dot{g}(\tau)=ag(\tau)-g(\tau-1)+\mathfrak{d}_{0}

in the sense of distributions, where
\mathfrak{d}_{0}
is the Dirac’s delta measure.

Lemma 3.17
.

The kernel for the RDE (
3.14
) is the function

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{gkernel}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}g(\tau)}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e^{a(\tau-k)}\frac{(\tau-k)^{k}}{k!}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[k,\infty)}(\tau).

Proof.

Indeed

\lim_{\tau\nearrow 0}g(\tau)=0,\quad\lim_{\tau\searrow 0}g(\tau)=1

and by differentiation for
\tau>0
we obtain

\displaystyle\dot{g}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}ae^{a(\tau-k)}\frac{(\tau-k)^{k}}{k!}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[k,\infty)}(\tau)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e^{a(\tau-k)}\frac{(\tau-k)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[k,\infty)}(\tau)

\displaystyle=ag(\tau)-g(\tau-1).\qed

Next we give the asymptotic behavior of
g
.

Lemma 3.18
.

It holds:

(1)

if
a>1
, then the kernel
g(\tau)
grows exponentially as

\frac{e^{\lambda_{0}^{+}\tau}}{1-e^{-\lambda_{0}^{+}}},

where
\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{-}
is the unique positive real eigenvalue of (
3.15
), and moreover
\tau\mapsto g(\tau)e^{-\lambda_{0}+\tau}-\frac{1}{1-e^{-\lambda_{0}^{+}}}
is exponentially decaying for
\tau\to\infty
;

(2)

if
a=1
, then

\tau\mapsto g(\tau)-2\tau+\frac{2}{3}

is exponentially decaying as
\tau\to\infty
;

(3)

if
a<1
, defining the quantity

U_{0}^{+}=\frac{1}{1-\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{+}}=|U_{0}^{+}|e^{i\ln(\Im(U_{0}^{+}))},

where
\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{+}
is the eigenvalue of (
3.15
) with minimal real part and positive imaginary part, we obtain that

\tau\mapsto\big[g(\tau)e^{-\Re(\lambda_{0}^{+})\tau}-2|U_{0}^{+}|\cos(\Im(s_{0}^{+})\tau+\ln(\Im(U_{0}^{+}))\big)\Big]

is exponentially decaying as
\tau\to\infty
.

Figure 18
.
Plot of
g(\tau)
with
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}-0.01
(green),
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}
(orange),
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}+0.1
(blue), and the asymptotic behavior of Point (2) of Lemma
3.18
.

Proof.

Take the Laplace transform
\hat{g}(s)
of
g(\tau)
: by using (
3.14
) we obtain

-g(0)+s\hat{g}(s)=a\hat{g}(s)-e^{-s}\hat{g}(s)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\hat{g}(s)=\frac{1}{s+e^{-s}-a}.

If
a\not=1
, then
\hat{g}
has a simple pole when

s+e^{-s}=a\quad\Longrightarrow\quad s=\lambda_{k},

where
\lambda_{k}
are the eigenvalues computed in the previous section.

For
a<1
, there is a couple of complex conjugate poles which have the least negative real part
\lambda_{0}^{\pm}=-x_{0}\pm iy_{0}
,
x_{0},y_{0}>0
: the residue about them is

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{U0pm}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}U_{0}^{\pm}}}=\frac{1}{\frac{d}{ds}\big(s+e^{-s}-a\big)\llcorner_{\lambda_{0}^{\pm}}}=\frac{1}{1-e^{-\lambda_{0}^{\pm}}}.

Defining

V

0

(τ)

\displaystyle=U_{0}^{-}e^{\lambda_{0}^{-}\tau}+U_{0}^{+}e^{\lambda_{0}^{+}\tau}

\displaystyle=2|U_{0}^{+}|e^{\Re(\lambda_{0}^{+})\tau}\cos(\Im(\lambda_{0}^{+})\tau+\Im(\ln(U_{0}^{+}))),

where

U_{0}^{+}=|U_{0}^{+}|e^{i\Im(\ln(U_{0}^{+}))},

we conclude that

\tau\mapsto(g(\tau)-V_{0}(\tau))e^{-\Re(\lambda_{0}^{+})\tau}

is exponentially decaying as
\tau\to+\infty
.

Same reasoning for
a>1
: in this case there is only one pole
\lambda_{0}^{+}
with positive real part, and then the residue is

U_{0}^{+}=\frac{1}{1-e^{-\lambda_{0}^{+}}}.

We thus conclude as before that

\tau\mapsto g(\tau)e^{-\lambda_{0}^{+}\tau}-U_{0}^{+}

is exponentially decaying as
\tau\to\infty
.

In the critical case, the Laplace transform has a pole in
0
of order
2
and can be expanded as

\hat{g}(s)=\frac{2}{s^{2}}\bigg(1+\frac{s}{3}+\mathcal{O}(s^{2})\bigg)=\frac{2}{s^{2}}+\frac{2}{3s}+\mathcal{O}(1).

Hence

g(\tau)-2\tau-\frac{2}{3}

decays exponentially as the first eigenvalue with negative real part for
\tau\to\infty
.
∎

In the following we need also to compute the solution
m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
for a Cauchy problem with a diffuse source: it will be useful when studying the fastest saturated spiral and its perturbations.

Lemma 3.19
.

The unique solution to the problem

(3.20)

\dot{\rho}(\tau)=a\rho(\tau)-\rho(\tau-1)+(2\pi+\alpha)1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau},\quad\rho(\tau)=0\ \text{for $\tau<\tau_{1}$},

where
c=c(\alpha)
is given by (
3.11
), is

m(τ,τ

1

,τ

2

)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Bigg[\frac{e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{1}}}{\cot(\alpha)}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}e^{a(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)}\frac{(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)^{\ell}}{\ell!((2\pi+\alpha)\cot(\alpha))^{k-\ell}}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{2}}}{\cot(\alpha)}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}e^{a(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)}\frac{(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)^{\ell}}{\ell!(\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha))^{k-\ell}}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\frac{e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{1}}}{\cot(\alpha)}[e^{a(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)}-1]^{+}-\frac{e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{2}}}{\cot(\alpha)}[e^{a(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)}-1]^{+}}{(\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha))^{k}}\Bigg].

Proof.

Using Duhamel’s formula we get that the solution is

m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=g\ast\rho_{0}(\tau),\quad\rho_{0}(\tau)=(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}(\tau),

that is

\displaystyle m(\tau,

\displaystyle\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=(2\pi+\alpha)\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}g(\tau-\tau^{\prime})e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau^{\prime}}d\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(2\pi+\alpha)\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e^{a(\tau-\tau^{\prime}-k)-(\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)-a)\tau^{\prime}}\frac{(\tau-\tau^{\prime}-k)^{k}}{k!}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[k,\infty)}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(2\pi+\alpha)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-k)}\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}(-1)^{k}e^{\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-\tau^{\prime}-k)}\frac{(\tau-\tau^{\prime}-k)^{k}}{k!}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[k,\infty)}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-k)}}{(\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha))^{1+k}}\int_{\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)}^{\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)}(-1)^{k}e^{\sigma}\frac{\sigma^{k}}{k!}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\sigma)d\sigma

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-k)}}{(\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha))^{1+k}}\bigg[\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}e^{\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)}\frac{(\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-\tau_{1}-k))^{\ell}}{\ell!}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\big[e^{\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)}-1\big]^{+}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}e^{\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)}\frac{(\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-\tau_{2}-k))^{\ell}}{\ell!}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\big[e^{\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha)(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)}-1\big]^{+}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\bigg[\frac{e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{1}}}{\cot(\alpha)}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}e^{a(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)}\frac{(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)^{\ell}}{\ell!((2\pi+\alpha)\cot(\alpha))^{k-\ell}}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{2}}}{\cot(\alpha)}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}e^{a(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)}\frac{(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)^{\ell}}{\ell!(\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha))^{k-\ell}}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\frac{e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{1}}}{\cot(\alpha)}[e^{a(\tau-\tau_{1}-k)}-1]^{+}-\frac{e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{2}}}{\cot(\alpha)}[e^{a(\tau-\tau_{2}-k)}-1]^{+}}{(\cot(\alpha)(2\pi+\alpha))^{k}}\bigg],

which is the formula in the statement.
∎

We compute the asymptotic behavior of
m
, which is the integral of the convolution of the asymptotic formula of
g
with the source
\rho_{0}
.

Figure 19
.
The kernel
m(\tau,\frac{1}{2},1)
with
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}-0.1
(green),
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}
(orange),
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}+0.1
(blue), and its oscillations about the asymptotic expansion of
m(\tau,\tau_{1},1)
for
\tau_{1}\in[\frac{1}{2},1]
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Denoting with
g^{\mathrm{asympt}}(\tau)
the asymptotic expansions of
g(\tau)
in Lemma
3.18
, it holds

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{mkernelasympt}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m^{\mathrm{asympt}}(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}}=\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\rho_{0}(\tau^{\prime})\ast g^{\mathrm{asympt}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}.

In particular, it is blowing up exponentially for
a>1
, decaying exponentially and oscillating for
a<1
and in the critical case
a=1
we have the asymptotic linear growth

\displaystyle m^{\mathrm{asympt}}(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\sim\bigg(2\tau+\frac{2}{3}\bigg)\frac{e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{1}}-e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{2}}}{\bar{c}}

\displaystyle\quad-2\frac{[\bar{c}(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{1}+1]e^{-\bar{s}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{1}}-[\bar{c}(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{2}+1]e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{2}}}{\bar{c}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\Re(s_{1}^{\pm})\tau}),

with
s_{1}^{\pm}
the first non
0
eigenvalues.

Proof.

Integrating

\displaystyle m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\sim\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau^{\prime}}\bigg(2(\tau-\tau^{\prime})+\frac{2}{3}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\Re(s_{1}^{\pm})(\tau-\tau^{\prime})})\bigg)d\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bigg(2\tau+\frac{2}{3}\bigg)\int_{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{1}}^{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{2}}e^{-\bar{c}\sigma}d\sigma-2\int_{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{1}}^{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{2}}\sigma e^{-\bar{c}\sigma}d\sigma+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\Re(s_{1}^{\pm})\tau})

\displaystyle=\bigg(2\tau+\frac{2}{3}\bigg)\frac{e^{-\bar{s}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{1}}-e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{2}}}{\bar{c}}

\displaystyle\quad-2\frac{[\bar{c}(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{1}+1]e^{-\bar{s}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{1}}-[\bar{c}(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{2}+1]e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau_{2}}}{\bar{c}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\Re(s_{1}^{\pm})\tau}),

which is the statement.
∎

Remark 3.21
.

In the original variables
r,\phi
, by (
3.12
) the kernel
G(\phi)
is computed as

G(ϕ)

\displaystyle=g\bigg(\frac{\phi}{2\pi+\alpha}\bigg)e^{c\phi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e^{\cot(\alpha)\phi-ak}\frac{(\frac{\phi}{2\pi+\alpha}-k)^{k}}{k!}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{(0,\infty]}\bigg(\frac{\phi}{2\pi+\alpha}-k\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e^{\cot(\alpha)(\phi-(2\pi+\alpha)k)}\frac{(\phi-(2\pi+\alpha)k)^{k}}{k!\sin(\alpha)^{k}}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\phi-(2\pi+\alpha)k),

where we used (
3.11
). We recall that this means in distributions

\dot{G}(\phi)=\cot(\alpha)G(\phi)-\frac{G(\phi-(2\pi+\alpha))}{\sin(\alpha)}+\mathfrak{d}_{0}.

In the original variables
r,\phi
the Cauchy problem with source (
3.20
) corresponds to

(3.21)

\dot{r}(\phi)=\cot(\alpha)r(\phi)-\frac{r(\phi-2\pi-\alpha)}{\sin(\alpha)}+1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[\phi_{1},\phi_{2})},

and the solution is

M(ϕ,ϕ

1

,ϕ

2

)

\displaystyle=e^{c\phi}m\bigg(\frac{\phi}{2\pi+\alpha},\frac{\phi_{1}}{2\pi+\alpha},\frac{\phi_{2}}{2\pi+\alpha}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\bigg[\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}e^{\cot(\alpha)(\phi-\phi_{1}-(2\pi+\alpha)k)}\frac{(\phi-\phi_{1}-(2\pi+\alpha)k)^{\ell}}{\ell!\sin(\alpha)^{k}\cot(\alpha)^{k+1-\ell}}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\phi-\phi_{1}-(2\pi+\alpha)k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}e^{\cot(\alpha)(\phi-\phi_{2}-(2\pi+\alpha)k)}\frac{(\phi-\phi_{2}-(2\pi+\alpha)k)^{\ell}}{\ell!\sin(\alpha)^{k}\cot(\alpha)^{k+1-\ell}}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\phi-\phi_{2}-(2\pi+\alpha)k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{[e^{\cos(\alpha)(\phi-\phi_{1}-(2\pi+\alpha)k)}-1]^{+}-[e^{\cot(\alpha)(\phi-\phi_{2}-(2\pi+\alpha)k)}-1]^{+}}{\cot(\alpha)}\bigg].

Remark 3.22
.

The following differential relations are elementary, but used very often in the next sections: we will thus collect them here as reference.

Derivative of
g,G
:

it holds

\frac{d}{d\tau}\big[\partial_{\phi_{0}}g(\tau-\tau_{0})\big]=a\big[\partial_{\tau_{0}}g(\tau-\tau_{0})\big]-\big[\partial_{\tau_{0}}g(\tau-\tau_{0}-1)\big]-\frac{d}{d\tau_{0}}\mathfrak{d}_{\tau_{0}},

\partial_{\tau_{0}}g(\tau-\tau_{0})=-\mathfrak{d}_{\tau_{0}}-ag(\tau-\tau_{0})+g(\tau-\tau_{0}-1).

Similarly for
G

\frac{d}{d\phi}\big[\partial_{\phi_{0}}G(\phi-\phi_{0})\big]=\cot(\alpha)\big[\partial_{\phi_{0}}G(\phi-\phi_{0})\big]-\frac{\big[\partial G(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\alpha)\big]}{\sin(\alpha)}+\frac{d}{d\phi_{0}}\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{0}},

(3.22)

\partial_{\phi_{0}}G(\phi-\phi_{0})=-\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{0}}-\cot(\alpha)G(\phi-\phi_{0})+\frac{G(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\alpha)}{\sin(\alpha)}.

Derivative of
m,M
:

it holds

\frac{d}{d\tau}\big[\partial_{\tau_{1}}m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\big]=a\big[\partial_{\tau_{1}}m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\big]-\big[\partial_{\tau_{1}}m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\big]-(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{1}}\mathfrak{d}_{\tau_{1}},

\partial_{\tau_{1}}m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=-(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{1}}g(\tau-\tau_{1}),

\frac{d}{d\tau}\big[\partial_{\tau_{2}}m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\big]=a\big[\partial_{\tau_{2}}m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\big]-\big[\partial_{\tau_{2}}m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\big]+(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{2}}\mathfrak{d}_{\tau_{2}},

\partial_{\tau_{2}}m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=(2\pi+\alpha)e^{-c(2\pi+\alpha)\tau_{2}}g(\tau-\tau_{2}).

Similarly for
M

\frac{d}{d\phi}\big[\partial_{\phi_{1}}M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\big]=\cot(\alpha)\big[\partial_{\phi_{1}}M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\big]-\frac{\big[\partial_{\phi_{1}}M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\big]}{\sin(\alpha)}-\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{1}},

(3.23)

\big[\partial_{\phi_{1}}M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\big]=-G(\phi-\phi_{1}),

\frac{d}{d\phi}\big[\partial_{\phi_{2}}M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\big]=\cot(\alpha)\big[\partial_{\phi_{2}}M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\big]-\frac{\big[\partial_{\phi_{2}}M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\big]}{\sin(\alpha)}+\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{2}},

\big[\partial_{\phi_{2}}M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\big]=G(\phi-\phi_{2}).

For the sake of completeness, in the critical case
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}
we give the asymptotic expansions of
G(\phi-\phi_{0})
and
M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
.

Corollary 3.23
.

When
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}
the following expansions hold

G(\phi-\phi_{0})=(\phi-\bar{\phi}_{0})e^{\bar{c}(\phi-\bar{\phi}_{0})}\bigg(1+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\phi-\bar{\phi}_{0}}\bigg)\bigg),

M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=2\frac{1-e^{\bar{c}(\phi_{2}=\phi_{1})}}{\bar{c}}(\phi-\phi_{1})e^{\bar{c}(\phi-\phi_{1})}\bigg(1+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\phi-\phi_{1}}\bigg)\bigg).

Proof.

It is an application of the asymptotic expansions for
g(\tau)
and
m(\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
.
∎

3.2.4.
Length of saturated spirals

For later use, we now compute the length of a saturated spiral, namely the quantity

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Gcal}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{G}(\phi)}}=\int_{-\infty}^{\phi}\frac{G(\phi^{\prime})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}d\phi^{\prime}=\int_{0}^{\tau}g(\tau^{\prime})e^{\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})(\tau^{\prime}+1)}d\tau^{\prime},\quad\phi=(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau,

satisfying the RDE

(3.24)

\frac{d}{d\phi}\mathcal{G}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\mathcal{G}(\phi)-\frac{\mathcal{G}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}+\frac{H(\phi)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})},

obtained by computing the expression
\frac{d^{2}}{d\phi^{2}}\mathcal{G}(\phi)
,
where
H(\phi)
is the Heaviside function

H(\phi)=\begin{cases}0&x<0,\\
1&x\geq 0.\end{cases}

In terms of
\tau
we will write also

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{gfrak}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathfrak{g}(\tau)}}=\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}g(\tau^{\prime})e^{\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})(\tau^{\prime}+1)}d\tau^{\prime}.

Using the formula

\int_{0}^{x}(-1)^{k}\frac{e^{x^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})^{k}}{k!}dx^{\prime}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}\frac{e^{x}x^{\ell}}{\ell!}-1,

we obtain the explicit formulas

\mathcal{G}(\phi)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\cos(\bar{\alpha})^{k+1}}\bigg[-1+\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-1)^{j}e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})k)}\frac{(\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})k))^{j}}{j!}\bigg]1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[0,\infty)}(\phi-(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})k).

For the kernel
M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
,

M\bigg(\phi,\phi_{0}+2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2},\phi_{0}+2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi\bigg)=\int_{\phi_{0}+2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}}^{\phi_{0}+2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi}G(\phi-\phi^{\prime})d\phi^{\prime}.

we have with simple calculus

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\phi}\frac{M(\phi^{\prime},\phi_{1},\phi_{2})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}d\phi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\phi}\bigg[\int_{\phi_{1}}^{\phi^{\prime}}-\int_{\phi_{2}}^{\phi^{\prime}}\bigg]\frac{G(\phi^{\prime}-\phi^{\prime\prime})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}d\phi^{\prime\prime}d\phi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\int_{0}^{\phi-\phi_{1}}-\int_{0}^{\phi-\phi_{2}}\bigg]\int_{0}^{\phi^{\prime}}\frac{G(\phi^{\prime}-\phi^{\prime\prime})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}d\phi^{\prime\prime}d\phi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})^{2}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\bigg[\int_{0}^{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})(\tau-\tau_{1})}-\int_{0}^{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})(\tau-\tau_{2})}\bigg]\int_{0}^{\tau^{\prime}}g(\tau^{\prime}-\tau^{\prime\prime})e^{\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})(\tau^{\prime}-\tau^{\prime\prime})}d\tau^{\prime}d\tau^{\prime\prime},

where we have used the change of variable
\phi=(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau
. Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{G}(\phi)
and defining

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Gfrak}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathfrak{G}(\tau)}}=\int_{0}^{\phi}\mathcal{G}(\phi^{\prime})d\phi^{\prime}=(2\pi+\alpha)\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathcal{G}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}=(2\pi+\alpha)\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau^{\prime}}g(\tau^{\prime}-\tau^{\prime\prime})e^{\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})(\tau^{\prime}-\tau^{\prime\prime}+1)}d\tau^{\prime}d\tau^{\prime\prime},

\begin{split}\mathfrak{G}(\phi)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\cos(\bar{\alpha})^{k+1}}\Bigg\{&-(\phi-(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})k)\\
&+\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{1}{\cot(\bar{\alpha})}\bigg[-1+\sum_{\ell=0}^{j}(-1)^{\ell}e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})k)}\frac{(\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})k))^{\ell}}{\ell!}\bigg]\Bigg\}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{\phi\geq(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})k}(\phi).\end{split}

we obtain thus

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\phi}\frac{M(\phi^{\prime},\phi_{1},\phi_{2})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}d\phi^{\prime}=\big(\mathfrak{G}(\tau-\tau_{1})-\mathfrak{G}(\tau-\tau_{2})\big),\quad\tau_{1}=\frac{\phi_{1}}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}},\ \tau_{2}=\frac{\phi_{2}}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}.

Finally by (
3.24
) we obtain

(3.25)

\frac{d}{d\phi}\mathfrak{G}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\mathfrak{G}(\phi)-\frac{\mathfrak{G}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}+\frac{\max\{0,\phi\}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}.

4.
Existence of optimal closing barriers at a given angle

One of the difficulties we face when studying this problem is that the optimization problem (
1.6
) may be empty (which is actually what we want to prove for
\sigma\leq\bar{\sigma}
). Aim of this section is to give a different minimization problem (Definition
4.3
) on the set of admissible spirals with the following property: if the minimum is
0
or negative then there is a spiral which is blocking the fire spreading.

Let
Z
be a given admissible spiral strategy, let
\bar{L}
be a large constant and let
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{phi0}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\phi_{0}}},\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{phibar}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{\phi}}}
be some rotation angles with
0\leq{\phi_{0}}\leq{\bar{\phi}}
.

Definition 4.1
.

The
set of admissible continuations

\mathcal{A}_{S}(Z,{\phi_{0}})
is the set of admissible spirals that coincide with
Z
up to the rotation angle
{\phi_{0}}
, can be prolonged to the rotation angle
{\bar{\phi}}
and have length uniformly bounded by
\bar{L}
.

Remark 4.2
.

In the previous definition the parameters
\bar{L},{\bar{\phi}}
do not appear in the notation
\mathcal{A}_{S}(Z,{\phi_{0}})
because, if there is a blocking spiral, then
\bar{L}
and
{\bar{\phi}}
can be chosen so that it belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{S}(Z,{\phi_{0}})
.

The parameter
\bar{L}
is needed because we need some compactness when using the angle variable
\phi
in the representation of the spiral: clearly
\bar{L}\to\infty
suggests that we are not blocking the fire. An estimate of the parameter
L
can be done by using the saturated spiral of Section
3.1
, which is always admissible, but not optimal: in particular there is at least on
L
such that the set
\mathcal{A}_{S}(Z,\phi_{0})
is not empty.

In Section
8
we will construct explicitly the optimal solution, thus obtaining also an estimate on
\bar{L}
: we remark that it will have a longer length than the saturated spiral. This spiral is optimal among all other admissible spirals, and then the parameter
L
will not play any role in the minimization problem below.

Definition 4.3

(Minimization problem)
.

We consider the following minimization problem:

(4.1)

\min\big\{r_{\tilde{Z}}({\bar{\phi}}),\tilde{Z}\in\mathcal{A}_{S}(Z,{\phi_{0}})\big\},

where
(r_{\tilde{Z}}(\phi),\phi)
is the angle-ray parametrization of the spiral
\tilde{Z}
.

Aim of this section is to prove that there is an optimal spiral for the problem (
4.1
) (for some
L\gg 1
fixed), and to construct the optimal solution in the case
\bar{\phi}<2\pi+\phi_{0}+\beta^{-}(\phi_{0})
.

One of the main ingredient is a compactness result in
\mathcal{A}_{S}(Z,{\phi_{0}})
. This compactness is independent of
{\phi_{0}}
, in the sense that
\mathcal{A}_{S}(Z,\phi_{0})
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{A}_{S}
. Hence we state it for the latter: this is slightly more technical because of the presence of the initial segment, where the angle-ray parametrization degenerates. It can be clearly adapted to become an independent proof of the existence of an optimal admissible spiral.

4.1.
Study of the minimization problem

Given the control parameter
\beta:[{\phi_{0}},{\bar{\phi}})\to(0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
with the convexity condition (
3.6
), the corresponding spiral is obtained by solving

(4.2)

Dr=\cot(\beta)r\mathscr{L}^{1}-Ds^{-},\quad r(0)=r_{0}\geq 1,

where
s^{-}(\phi)
is constructed according to Proposition
3.5
, i.e. at the previous round. The initial segment is
[(1,0),(r_{0},0)]
, which we assume to belong to the initial given spiral arc: we will see that it is not the optimal solution.

The set where the functions
\beta(\phi)
can be chosen is the intersection of the two sets

\begin{split}&\bigg\{\beta:\forall\phi\in[0,{\bar{\phi}}]\bigg(\mathcal{A}(\phi)=1+s^{-}(\phi)+r(\phi)-\frac{s^{+}(\phi)}{\sigma}\geq 0\bigg)\bigg\}\cap\bigg\{\beta\in\bigg(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg]:r(\phi)>0,D\beta+1\geq 0,r_{0}-1+s^{+}(\phi)\leq\bar{L}\bigg\}.\end{split}

The first set is the set of admissible spirals, while the second means that the solution to (
4.2
) is a spiral, that the spiral is convex, and that its total length is less than
\bar{L}
. The condition

\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\frac{1}{\beta(\phi)}d\phi<\infty

is hidden in the requirement that the total length is bounded and the curve arrives at angle
{\bar{\phi}}>0
, see Proposition
3.5
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Consider a sequence of
\beta^{n}(\phi)
and initial segments
[(0,0),(r_{0}^{n},0)]
such that

(4.3)

\beta^{n}\in\bigg(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg],\quad D\beta^{n}+1\geq 0,\quad\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\frac{1}{\beta^{n}(\phi)}d\phi<\infty,

and let
\zeta^{n}
be the spiral constructed by Proposition
3.5
by solving (
3.3
). Assume that
L(\zeta^{n})\leq\bar{L}
and for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that

(4.4)

r^{n}(\phi)\geq\delta\quad\forall\phi\in(\epsilon,{\bar{\phi}}-\epsilon).

Then there is a subsequence (not relabeled) such that
\beta^{n}\to\beta
,
r^{n}\to r
in
L^{1}(0,{\bar{\phi}})
and
\zeta^{n}\to\zeta
in
W^{1,1}(0,{\bar{\phi}})
, with
\zeta(0)-1+L(\zeta)\leq\bar{L}
.

The uniform positivity of
r^{n}
implies the curves
\zeta^{n}
are not converging to a curve which closes before the angle
{\bar{\phi}}
, thus making the angle-ray description meaningless. For the fire blocking problem this
{\bar{\phi}}
could be every angle before the minimal angle for which there is a blocking spiral.

Proof.

We start with a series of compactness estimates. At each step we will extract the corresponding converging subsequence: we will often avoid relabeling the subsequences.

Step 1. The total variation of
\beta^{n}
is uniformly controlled by

\text{\rm Tot.Var.}(\beta^{n},[0,{\bar{\phi}}])\leq\frac{\pi}{2}+2{\bar{\phi}}.

This follows from the one-sided Lipschitz condition
D\beta^{n}+1\geq 0
and
\beta\in[0,\pi/2]
. Hence, being
\beta^{n}\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
bounded, we can assume that
\beta^{n}\to\beta
in
L^{1}(0,{\bar{\phi}})
: moreover the points where it is not convergent are the discontinuity points of
\beta
, which form a countable family.

Step 2.
Having finite length, it follows that the final points
(r^{n}_{0},0)
of the initial segment are compact, and then
r^{n}_{0}\to r_{0}
. This is not in general the final point of the initial segment, because
\zeta^{n}
may add additional intervals to this segment.

Step 3.
The unit vectors
\dot{\zeta}^{n}
rotate monotonically about
0
for a total angle
\leq{\bar{\phi}}+\frac{\pi}{2}
, so that the measure second derivative of
\zeta^{n}
satisfies

|D^{2}\zeta^{n}|\leq{\bar{\phi}}+\frac{\pi}{2}.

Hence we can assume that
\zeta^{n}\to\zeta
in
W^{1,1}(0,{\bar{\phi}})
, and also
D^{2}\zeta^{n}\rightharpoonup D^{2}\zeta
weakly on
(0,{\bar{\phi}})
.

Step 4.
Since
\dot{\zeta}^{n}\to\dot{\zeta}
in
L^{1}(0,{\bar{\phi}})
, the monotone function
\phi\mapsto s^{-,n}(\phi)
converges in
L^{1}(0,{\bar{\phi}})
to the corresponding function
\phi\mapsto s^{-}(\phi)
: indeed
s^{-,n}(\phi)\to s^{-}(\phi)
in every point
\phi
where the latter is continuous. In particular
Ds^{-,n}\rightharpoonup Ds^{-}
weakly in the interval
(0,{\bar{\phi}})
. Observe also that
\text{\rm Tot.Var.}(s^{-,n},[0,{\bar{\phi}}])\leq\bar{L}
.

Step 5.
By the length formula (
3.2
), using the one-sided Lipschitz condition (
4.3
) for
\beta^{n}
and the fact that
r^{n}(\phi)
is uniformly positive for
\phi\in(\epsilon,{\bar{\phi}}-\epsilon)
by (
4.4
) and bounded above by the
\bar{L}+1
, we conclude that there exists
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
such that
\beta^{n}(\phi)\geq\epsilon^{\prime}
for all
\phi\in(\epsilon,{\bar{\phi}}-\epsilon)
. Hence

\frac{1}{\sin(\beta^{n}(\phi))}\to\frac{1}{\sin(\beta(\phi))}\quad\text{in}\ L^{1}(\epsilon,{\bar{\phi}}-\epsilon),\ \forall\epsilon>0.

Since for
\phi\in[0,2\pi]
it holds
r^{n}(\phi)\geq 1
, we also conclude that

\frac{\mathscr{L}^{1}}{\sin(\beta^{n})}\rightharpoonup c\mathfrak{d}_{0}+\frac{\mathscr{L}^{1}}{\sin(\beta)}\quad\text{weakly in}\ [0,{\bar{\phi}}-\delta],\ \forall\delta>0.

Observe also that

\cot(\beta^{n})\mathscr{L}^{1}\rightharpoonup c\mathfrak{d}_{0}+\cot(\beta)\mathscr{L}^{1}\quad\text{weakly in}\ [0,{\bar{\phi}}-\delta],\ \forall\delta>0,

because
\cot(\beta)\sim\frac{1}{\sin(\beta)}
when
\beta\searrow 0
.

Step 6.
Consider now the ODE (
3.3
): since

D(r^{n}+s^{-,n})=\cot(\beta^{n})r^{n}\geq 0,\quad 1\leq r^{n}\leq\bar{L},

it follows that

\text{\rm Tot.Var.}(r^{n},[0,{\bar{\phi}}])\leq 2\bar{L}.

Hence also
r^{n}\to r
in
L^{1}(0,{\bar{\phi}})
.

Step 7.
The ODE (
3.3
) passes to the limit as

Dr=\cot(\beta)r\mathscr{L}^{1}-Ds^{-},\quad\phi\in(0,{\bar{\phi}}),

where we observed that
s^{-,n}(\phi)=0
for
\phi\in[0,2\pi)
. It remains to analyze the initial data, where a Dirac’s delta can accumulate.

Using the estimates of Step 5 for
\phi\to 0
and that
s^{-,n}=0
in the first round, we can compute

r^{n}(\phi)=r^{n}_{0}e^{\int_{0}^{\phi}\cot(\beta^{n})\mathscr{L}^{1}}.

Passing to the limit we obtain

r(\phi)=(r_{0}e^{c})e^{\int_{0}^{\phi}\cot(\beta)\mathscr{L}^{1}},

so that the initial segment for the spiral is
[(1,0),(r_{0}e^{c},0)]
.

Step 8.
Finally, we show that the limit curve
\zeta
is represented by the limit
r(\phi)
. First one observes that from

\frac{ds^{+,n}}{d\phi}=\frac{r^{n}(\phi)}{\sin(\beta^{n}(\phi))}

and the convergence of
\frac{\mathscr{L}^{1}}{\sin(\beta^{n})}
of Step 5 and of
r^{n}
, it follows that
s^{+,n}
converges uniformly in
(\delta,{\bar{\phi}}-\delta)
.

One then passes to the limit

\zeta^{n}(s^{+,n}(\phi))=\zeta^{n}(s^{-,n}(\phi))+r^{n}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi},

obtaining that for
\mathscr{L}^{1}
-a.e.
\phi

\zeta(s^{+}(\phi))=\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+r(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}.

The remaining
\phi
are obtained by taking the limit from the right (we want
r(\phi)
to be right continuous) and the angle
{\bar{\phi}}
is obtained by taking the limit
\phi\nearrow{\bar{\phi}}
: we are using
D\zeta=e^{\imath\phi}D(s^{-}+r)+\imath e^{i\phi}r
, which gives that the limits exist because
D(r+s^{-})\in L^{\infty}
(and the curve is Lipschitz).

Step 9.
In this last step we just verify the length estimate: again using the convergence estimates of Steps 5, 7 and 8 we obtain

r_{0}^{n}-1+s^{+,n}(\phi)\quad\to\quad(r_{0}e^{c})-1+s^{+}(\phi),

so that the length bound depends continuously.
∎

We single out the convergences we obtained in the proof.

Corollary 4.5
.

Under the assumptions of the previous proposition, we can extract a subsequence
\beta^{n}
such that:

(1)

the functions
\beta^{n}
,
r^{n}
,
s^{-,n}
are uniformly BV and converge in
L^{1}(0,{\bar{\phi}})
to
\beta,r,s^{-}
;

(2)

the functions
s^{+,n},r^{n}+s^{-,n}
converges locally uniformly in
(0,{\bar{\phi}})
to
s^{+},r+s^{-}
;

(3)

the spirals
\zeta^{n}
converges in
W^{1,1}(0,{\bar{\phi}})
to a spiral
\zeta
, corresponding to the angle-ray representation
r
with control angle
\beta
;

(4)

the time function
u
is converging locally uniformly in the set
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\zeta([0,{\bar{\phi}}])
if
r({\bar{\phi}})>0
, otherwise converges locally uniformly in the open set
\cup_{\phi}\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+e^{\imath\phi}(0,r(\phi))
.

Hence the admissibility functional
\mathcal{A}(\phi)
for
\zeta^{n}
converges to the admissibility functional for
\zeta
for all
\phi>0
.

In particular, if
\zeta^{n}
are admissible, so it is
\zeta
.
