Proof.

The convergence of the admissibility functional follows from the uniform convergence of
s^{+,n},r^{n}+s^{-,n}
.

We now study the convergence of the time function
u
. By the uniform convergence of
\zeta^{n}
, it is enough to study the behavior of
u
in each round, i.e. the convergence of the time function (
2.11
) when the region
S_{\ell}^{n}
is bounded by a simple curve converging in
W^{1,1}
(the case after the last round (where
u
has to be computed outside
S_{\ell}^{n}
) can be easily be handled in a similar way). The weak convergence of
D^{2}\zeta^{n}
implies that the map

x\mapsto s^{n}(x)\quad\text{such that}\quad\frac{x-\zeta^{n}(s^{n}(x))}{|x-\zeta^{n}(s^{n}(x))|}\in\partial^{-}\zeta^{n}(s^{n}(x))\ \text{and}\ (\zeta^{n})^{-1}((\zeta^{n}(s^{n}(x)),x))=\emptyset

converges pointwise, and then locally uniformly. By repeating this reasoning for the finite number of rounds we obtain the statement on
u
.
∎

We can now state the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 4.6
.

Fix a rotation angle
{\bar{\phi}}\geq{\phi_{0}}
and a length
\bar{L}
sufficiently large. Then either there exists an admissible spiral in
\mathcal{A}_{S}(Z,{\phi_{0}})
blocking the fire before or at
{\bar{\phi}}
or there exists an admissible spiral
\zeta
such that
r({\bar{\phi}})
is minimal among all admissible spirals of length bounded by
\bar{L}
.

Remark 4.7
.

In the main theorem of the paper we will give an explicit form for the optimal spiral, which is independent on the parameter
\bar{L}
, so that in particular the length
\bar{L}
where to search for the minimum can be explicitly estimated.

Proof.

If there is an admissible spiral blocking the fire before or at
{\bar{\phi}}
there is nothing to prove. Otherwise consider the number

\inf\Big\{r({\bar{\phi}})=\big|\zeta(s^{+}({\bar{\phi}}))-\zeta(s^{-}({\bar{\phi}}))\big|,\zeta\in\mathcal{A}_{S}(Z,{\phi_{0}})\Big\}.

Notice that the set is not empty if
\bar{L}\gg 1
, since it contains the saturated spiral of the previous section, whose length is bounded by
e^{\cot(\alpha)\bar{\phi}}
.

If this number if
0
, then there are a sequence of admissible spirals
\zeta^{n}
such that

\zeta^{n}(s^{+,n}({\bar{\phi}}))-\zeta^{n}(s^{-,n}({\bar{\phi}}))\to 0.

Hence by the convergence of Corollary
4.5
it follows that up to subsequences
\zeta^{n}
converges to a spiral such that

\lim_{\phi\nearrow{\phi_{0}}}\zeta(s^{+}({\bar{\phi}}))-\zeta(s^{-}({\bar{\phi}}))=0,

so that it blocks the fire at
{\bar{\phi}}
.

The same reasoning proves that there exists a minimizer
\zeta
if the minimum is strictly positive.
∎

In the next sections we will address this optimization problem, and construct explicitly the optimal solution for spiral arcs corresponding to the first round after
\bar{\phi}_{0}
.

4.2.
Study of the distance function
u

In this subsection we study more carefully the time function
u
: we recall that the time function
u
is given by

u(x)=\min\big\{L(\gamma),\gamma\ \text{admissible}\big\}.

In this subsection we will prove that
u
is locally convex in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus Z
. This follows from the structure of
Z
, which is a connected curve. The normal to the level set is
\nabla u(x)=e^{\imath\phi(x)}
, where
\phi(x)
is the angle of the optimal ray passing through
x
. Moreover, the radius of curvature of the level set is
x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))
, i.e. the distance of the point
x
from the last point where the optimal ray touches the spiral.

We will prove these properties in Lemma
4.8
and Lemma
4.9
.

Lemma 4.8
.

The function
u
is of class
C^{1,1}
and locally convex in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus Z
. If

x\in\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+e^{\imath\phi}r(\phi),

then

\nabla u(x)=\frac{x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|}=\mathbf{t}(x),\quad\nabla^{2}u(x)=\frac{1}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|}\mathbf{n}(x)\otimes\mathbf{n}(x),\text{ a.e. }x,

with
\mathbf{n}(x)=\mathbf{t}(x)^{\perp}
,
\mathbf{t}(x)\in\partial^{-}\zeta(s^{-}(x))
.

Moreover
\nabla^{2}u
is locally BV with jumps on the directions where
Z
is flat, corresponding to the points where the radius of curvature
|x-\zeta(s(x))|
has a jump: more precisely

\begin{split}D\nabla^{2}u(x)\mathbf{t}(x)&=\bigg[\mathbf{t}(x)\cdot D\bigg(\frac{1}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|}\bigg)\bigg]\mathbf{n}(x)\otimes\mathbf{n}(x)=-\frac{\mathbf{n}(x)\otimes\mathbf{n}(x)}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|^{2}}\mathscr{L}^{2},\end{split}

\begin{split}D\nabla^{2}u(x)\mathbf{n}(x)&=-\bigg[\mathbf{n}(x)\cdot D\bigg(\frac{1}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|}\bigg)\bigg]\mathbf{n}(x)\otimes\mathbf{n}(x)-\frac{1}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi)|}\big(\mathbf{t}(x)\otimes\mathbf{n}(x)+\mathbf{n}(x)\otimes\mathbf{t}(x)\big)\mathscr{L}^{2}.\end{split}

Proof.

All statements follow from the computation of the formulas for
\nabla u
and
\nabla^{2}u
. Moreover, we can assume that
\zeta
is smooth with
s\mapsto e^{\imath\theta}
smooth with Lipschitz inverse, and then pass to the limit, because of the convergence of
s^{-}(\phi)
as in Corollary
4.5
.

In this case the function
u
is computed by the formula

u(\zeta(s)+\dot{\zeta}(s)r)=1+s+r.

Differentiating one has

\nabla u\cdot(\dot{\zeta}(s)+\ddot{\zeta}(s)r,\dot{\zeta}(s))=(1,1)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\nabla u(\zeta(s)+\dot{\zeta}(s)r)=\dot{\zeta}(s),

which gives the first formula, and

\nabla^{2}u:\dot{\zeta}(s)\otimes(\dot{\zeta}(s)+\ddot{\zeta}(s)r)+\nabla u\ddot{\zeta}(s)=0,

\nabla^{2}u:(\dot{\zeta}(s)+\ddot{\zeta}(s)r)\otimes(\dot{\zeta}(s)+\ddot{\zeta}(s)r)+\nabla u(\ddot{\zeta}(s)+\dddot{\zeta}(s)r)=0,

which, together with

\nabla^{2}u:\dot{\zeta}(s)\otimes\dot{\zeta}(s)=0,\quad\dot{\zeta}(s)\cdot\dddot{\zeta}(s)=-\frac{1}{R^{2}},

gives the second formula, with
R
local radius of curvature of
\zeta
.

Taking one further derivative one obtains

\partial_{r}\nabla^{2}u=-\frac{\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n}}{r^{2}}.

\begin{split}\partial_{s}\nabla^{2}u&=\partial_{s}\frac{\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n}}{r}=-\frac{1}{r}\big(\ddot{\zeta}^{\perp}\otimes\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}\otimes\ddot{\zeta}^{\perp}\big),\end{split}

We thus obtain

\nabla^{3}u\cdot\mathbf{t}=-\frac{\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n}}{r^{2}},

\nabla^{3}u\cdot\mathbf{n}=\frac{R}{r^{3}}\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\mathbf{t}\otimes\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{t}).

Using that
\mathbf{t}=e^{\imath\theta}
and

\frac{d\phi}{ds}=\frac{1}{R},\quad R=\frac{ds}{d\phi},

\begin{split}\nabla\bigg(\frac{1}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|}\bigg)&=-\frac{\mathbf{t}(x)}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|^{2}}-\frac{R}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|^{3}}\mathbf{n}(x),\end{split}

we get the formula in the statement, because when
\frac{ds}{d\theta}
is singular then
\theta(s)
is continuous and thus also both
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{n}
are continuous there.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

The level sets of the minimum time function
u
are
\mathcal{C}^{1,1}
curves, with second derivative BV.

Proof.

Let
x(\upsilon)
be a parametrization by arc length of the level set
u=\text{const}
. Then differentiating
u\circ x(\upsilon)=\text{const}
, under the assumption that
\upsilon\mapsto e^{\imath\theta}
is Lipschitz with Lipschitz inverse we obtain

\nabla u\cdot\dot{x}(\upsilon)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\dot{x}(\upsilon)=\mathbf{n},

\nabla^{2}u:\dot{x}\times\dot{x}+\nabla u\ddot{x}=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\ddot{x}=-\frac{\mathbf{t}}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|},

\dddot{x}=\nabla\bigg(-\frac{\mathbf{t}}{|x-\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))|}\bigg)\mathbf{n}=\frac{R}{r^{3}}\mathbf{t}-\frac{\mathbf{n}}{r^{2}},

which gives the statement as in the previous proof because
\nabla^{2}u
is locally BV.
∎

4.3.
Construction of the curve of minimal reachable points for the first round

In this section we will construct the optimal solution to Theorem
4.6
in the following situation: the spiral
Z=\zeta([0,S])\in\mathcal{A}_{S}
is fixed up to a rotation angle
{\phi_{0}}
, and we will assume that the angle
{\bar{\phi}}
in Theorem
4.6
belongs to the first round after
\phi_{0}
, i.e. it satisfies

0\leq{\bar{\phi}}-{\phi_{0}}\leq 2\pi+\beta^{-}({\phi_{0}}).

This constraint is easily explained: we optimize
r(\phi)
whenever the choice of
\beta(\phi)
,
\phi\in({\phi_{0}},{\bar{\phi}})
, is not having influence on the behavior of
Z
afterwards. In other words, we do not need to care about the delay behavior of the ODE, since
s^{-}(\phi)
is assigned by the fixed part of the spiral.

In this situation, the only constraints we have to abide are the monotonicity of
u\circ\zeta
, the admissibility and the convexity of the curve: these two constraints are sufficient to determine uniquely the solution. Indeed, the optimal solution will be made of at most these three parts, in sequence:

•

an arc of the level set of
u
passing through
\zeta({\phi_{0}})
,

•

a segment,

•

an arc of a saturated spiral.

These components are connected by requiring to be tangent at the junction points, and determine a family of convex curves. The curve of the end points
{\bar{\phi}}\mapsto\hat{\zeta}=\zeta({\bar{\phi}})
is the
optimality curve
, i.e. for each angle
{\bar{\phi}}
the value
r({\bar{\phi}})
(i.e. the length of the last segment of the optimal ray) is the minimal value of
r
in Theorem
4.6
, and the corresponding sequence of (level-set arc)-(segment)-(arc of saturated spiral) is the unique optimal barrier achieving the minimal
r({\bar{\phi}})
. The optimality curve determines a non-admissibility region, where no admissible barrier can be constructed, namely the region

\Big\{\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+(0,r(\bar{\phi}))e^{\imath\bar{\phi}},\bar{\phi}\in\phi_{0}+(0,2\pi+\beta^{-}(\phi_{0}))\Big\}.

Definition 4.10
.

The set
\mathcal{R}({\bar{\phi}})
of minimal reachable points (Figure
20
) is the set of end points of curves made of a piece of fire level set
\{u=u(\zeta({\phi_{0}}))\}
plus a tangent segment to the level set: all the points on these curves should be admissible, should not cross
Z
and the last point of these curves must be saturated (meaning that
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})=0
).

It is possible that none of the points on the fire level set
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xilevel}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\xi}}=u^{-1}(u(\zeta({\phi_{0}})))
is saturated, which means that an admissible strategy is to construct a wall on the whole level set
u^{-1}(u(\zeta({\phi_{0}}))
: in this case there is no starting point of the curve
\mathcal{R}(\bar{\zeta})
. We thus assume that there is a point
\xi(a)
,
a\geq 0
is the arc length parametrization, such that

u(\xi(a))=u(\zeta({\phi_{0}}))=\frac{L(\zeta([0,{\phi_{0}}]))+L(\xi([0,\bar{a}]))}{\sigma}=\frac{L(\zeta([0,{\phi_{0}}]))+a}{\sigma},

which is the saturation condition. Clearly such a point is unique, see Figure
20
, and will be the starting point of the curve
\mathcal{R}
.

Figure 20
.
The magenta line is the level set of the time function
u
passing through
\zeta({\phi_{0}})
, the green line is the set
\mathcal{R}
where all the points are saturated. It divides the region into the admissible and the non admissible part (gray). The yellow curve is the optimal solution for the angle
\bar{\phi}>\phi_{2}
: it is made by an arc of level set, a segment and an arc of the saturated spiral.

To construct this curve
\mathcal{R}
, we will write the tangent in each point. Let
x
be a point in
\mathcal{R}
, let
[\zeta(s^{-}(x)),x]
be the last part (a segment) of the optimal ray (with
x\mapsto\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{s(x)}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s^{-}(x)}}
being the map to the initial point of the last segment in the optimal ray), and
[\xi(a(x)),x]
be the segment part of the admissible curve (again
a(x)
is the point on
\xi
whose tangent passes through
x
), with respective length
r,b
: more precisely, using the same notation for the subdifferential for the convex curve
\xi
,

x=\zeta(s)+r\zeta^{\prime}(s)=\xi(a)+b\xi^{\prime}(a),\quad\zeta^{\prime}(s)\in\partial^{-}\zeta(s),\ \xi^{\prime}(a)\in\partial^{-}\xi(a).

The first equalities express that
x
belongs to the optimal ray and the half-line tangent to
\xi
, and the second equality is the tangency properties of the rays.

We will also denote with
u_{\xi}
the solution to the Eikonal equation with barrier
\xi
and value
u(\zeta(\phi_{0}))=0
, which is explicitly given by

u_{\xi}(\xi(a)+b\xi^{\prime}(a))=a+b.

We recall also that the function
u
is computed similarly as

u(\zeta(s)+r\zeta^{\prime}(s))=s+r.

Define also the vectors

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{tbf(x)}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbf{t}(x)}}=\nabla u(x)=\frac{x-\zeta(s)}{|x-\zeta(s)|},\ \lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{nbf(x)}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbf{n}(x)}}=\mathbf{t}^{\perp},\quad\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{tbfxi(x)}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbf{t}_{\xi}(x)}}=\dot{\xi}(a)=\frac{x-\xi(a)}{|x-\xi(a)|},\ \lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{nbfxi(x)}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbf{n}_{\xi}(x)}}=\mathbf{t}^{\perp}_{\xi}.

We first consider the curve

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Rtt}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{R}}}=\bigg\{x:u(x)-\frac{u_{\xi}(x)}{\sigma}=\frac{L(\zeta(0,{\phi_{0}}))}{\sigma}\bigg\}.

This curve coincides with
\mathcal{R}
if all the points in the segment
\xi(a)+(0,b)\xi^{\prime}(a)
are all admissible. By definition the points in
\mathtt{R}
are saturated, in the sense that the admissibility functional
\mathcal{A}
is
0
.

Proposition 4.11
.

The tangent curve
\mathtt{R}
in
x\in\mathtt{R}
is given by

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{rbftangRtt}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbf{r}(x)}}=\frac{\mathbf{n}(x)-\frac{\mathbf{n}_{\xi}(x)}{\sigma}}{|\mathbf{n}(x)-\frac{\mathbf{n}_{\xi}(x)}{\sigma}|},

oriented according to
u\circ\mathtt{R},u_{\xi}\circ\mathtt{R}
increasing.

With this orientation, the angle
\angle(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{r})
is decreasing in the admissible region, and the last admissible point
x_{\mathrm{sl}}
of
\mathtt{R}
corresponds to the slope of the saturated spiral starting from that point (i.e. it forms an angle
\alpha
with the optimal ray of
\zeta
). After this point the curve
\mathtt{R}
is not admissible, in the sense that the segment
\xi(a)+(0,b)\xi^{\prime}(a)
has points outside the admissibility region (i.e.
\mathcal{A}<0
on some point of the segment
\xi(a)+b\xi^{\prime}(a)
).

Finally, the curve is convex in the direction of
\mathbf{r}^{\perp}
in the admissible part.

Figure 21
.
Construction of the curve
\mathtt{R}
: the curve is made of saturated points when the spiral is prolonged by an arc of the level set and the blue segment. The last admissibility point is when
\mathtt{R}
is tangent to the saturated spiral, and after that point it is not admissible.

Proof.

Using the explicit formula for the two solutions of the Eikonal equation with barrier
\zeta,\xi
,

u(\zeta(s)+r\zeta^{\prime}(s))=s+r,\quad u_{\xi}(\xi(a)+b\xi^{\prime}(a))=a+b,

the admissibility functional takes the form

\mathcal{A}(x)=s+r-\frac{a+b+L(\zeta(0,{\phi_{0}}))}{\sigma}=\text{const}+u(x)-\cos(\alpha)u_{\xi}(x)=0.

Differentiating and using Lemma
4.8
we obtain

\nabla\mathcal{A}=\mathbf{t}-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{t}_{\xi},

and then, being the points of
\mathtt{R}
saturated,

\big(\nabla u-\cos(\alpha)\nabla u_{\xi}\big)\cdot\mathbf{r}=\big(\mathbf{t}-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{t}_{\xi}\big)\cdot\mathbf{r}=0,

with
\textbf{r}=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{rbftangRtt}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbf{r}(x)}}
the vector of the statement (the tangent to the curve
\mathtt{R}
), and this gives precisely the first formula in the statement up to the orientation.

We compute the derivative of
u\circ\mathtt{R}
:

(u\circ\mathtt{R})^{\prime}=-\cos(\alpha)\frac{(\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi})}{|\mathbf{n}-\frac{\mathbf{n}_{\xi}}{\sigma}|}

which is positive because of the orientation of the vectors (see Figure
21
). The case when
\mathbf{t}\perp\mathbf{n}_{\xi}
means that the lines are parallel, and then
\mathtt{R}\to\infty
, which is impossible for
\sigma>1
. Similarly,

(u_{\xi}\circ\mathtt{R})^{\prime}=\frac{(\mathbf{t}_{\xi}\cdot\mathbf{n})}{|\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{n}_{\xi}/\sigma|}=-\frac{(\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi})}{|\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{n}_{\xi}/\sigma|}>0,

as the saturation condition requires. This concludes the first part of the proposition.

In the starting point on the level set
u^{-1}(\zeta({\phi_{0}}))
we obtain
\mathbf{n}_{\xi}=-\mathbf{t}
, so that the vector of the statement becomes

\mathbf{r}=\frac{\mathbf{n}+\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{t}}{|\mathbf{n}+\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{t}|}.

In particular the initial angle
\angle(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{t})
is greater than
\alpha
. In general, in the fire frame
(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{n})
the position of
\mathbf{n}-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{n}_{\xi}
is contained in the circle
(0,1)+\{|x|=\cos(\alpha)\}
, and the minimal slope is when
\mathbf{r}=e^{\imath\alpha}
. Writing the derivative of
(\nabla u\cdot\nabla u_{\xi})\circ\mathtt{R}
explicitly one gets by Lemma
4.8

\begin{split}((\mathbf{\nabla}u\cdot\nabla u_{\xi})\circ\mathtt{R})^{\prime}&=\mathbf{r}^{T}\frac{\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n}}{r}\mathbf{t}_{\xi}+\mathbf{r}^{T}\frac{\mathbf{n}_{\xi}\otimes\mathbf{n}_{\xi}}{b}\mathbf{t}\\
&=\frac{\mathbf{t}_{\xi}\cdot\mathbf{n}}{|\mathbf{n}-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{n}_{\xi}|}\bigg(\frac{1-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi}}{r}-\frac{\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi}-\cos(\alpha)}{b}\bigg)\\
&=\frac{\mathbf{t}_{\xi}\cdot\mathbf{n}}{|\mathbf{n}-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{n}_{\xi}|}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{r}+\frac{\cos(\alpha)}{b}\bigg)-((\mathbf{\nabla}u\cdot\nabla u_{\xi})\circ\mathtt{R})\bigg(\frac{\cos(\alpha)}{r}+\frac{1}{b}\bigg)\bigg].\end{split}

The quantity

\frac{1-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi}}{r}-\frac{\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi}-\cos(\alpha)}{b}

is positive for
b\ll 1
, because
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi}\searrow 0
as we approach the starting point, and whenever this quantity is positive the vectors
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t}_{\xi}
are approaching, which means that
\textbf{r}\cdot\textbf{t}
decreases. Note that at the minimal angle

((\nabla u\cdot\nabla u_{\xi})\circ\mathtt{R})^{\prime}=\frac{1-\cos^{2}(\alpha)}{r}>0,

so this behavior is valid up to the last admissible point
x_{\mathrm{sl}}
. This proves the first part of the second statement of the proposition. In the point
x_{\mathrm{sl}}
we have that
\mathbf{r}\parallel\mathbf{t}_{\xi}
.

After the critical angle, the vector
\mathbf{t}_{\xi}
moves counterclockwise having derivative
\propto\mathbf{n}_{\xi}(\mathbf{n}_{\xi}\cdot\mathbf{n}-\cos(\alpha))
, which means that we are going to take values of the parameter
a
(the starting point of the segment from the barrier
u^{-1}(u(\zeta({\phi_{0}})))
) already used.
However the gradient of the admissibility functional satisfies

\nabla\mathcal{A}=\mathbf{t}-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{t}_{\xi},

and thus for directions with angle
>\alpha

\nabla\mathcal{A}\cdot\mathbf{t}_{\xi}=\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{t}_{\xi}-\cos(\alpha)<0,

because
\nabla u\cdot\nabla u_{\xi}
is still increasing. Hence in this part of the curve the functional is not admissible. This concludes the second part of the proposition.

We are left to prove that
\mathtt{R}
is convex. The second derivative of
\mathcal{A}
along
\mathbf{v}
gives

\begin{split}\mathbf{r}^{T}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{A}\mathbf{r}&=\frac{(\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{r})^{2}}{r}-\cos(\alpha)\frac{(\mathbf{n}_{\xi}\cdot\mathbf{r})^{2}}{b}\\
&=\frac{1}{|\mathbf{n}-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{n}_{\xi}|^{2}}\bigg(\frac{(1-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi})^{2}}{r}-\frac{(\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi}-\cos(\alpha))^{2}}{b}\bigg)\\
&\geq\frac{1}{|\mathbf{n}-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{n}_{\xi}|^{2}}\frac{\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi}-\cos(\alpha)}{b}\big(1-\cos(\alpha)\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi}-\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\xi}+\cos(\alpha)\big)>0,\end{split}

where in the last line we have used the estimate on
((\nabla u\cdot\nabla u_{\xi})\circ\mathtt{R})^{\prime}>0
in the admissibility region. Hence the curve is convex in the direction of
\mathbf{r}^{\perp}
.
∎

Using the above proposition, we construct the optimal closing curve as follows, see Fig.
22
.

Case 1:

the level set
u^{-1}(u(\zeta({\phi_{0}}))
of the time function is always admissible, ending on a point of the spiral
Z
.

In this case for all angle
{\bar{\phi}}\geq{\phi_{0}}
the optimal closing curve will be the level set, and the optimality curve
\mathcal{R}
is the level set itself.

Case 2:

there are non admissible points on the level set: thus we can construct the curve
\mathtt{R}
, and this curve remains admissible until it touches again
Z
.

In this case, define the optimality curve as

\mathcal{R}=\big\{x:u(x)=u(\zeta({\phi_{0}})),\mathcal{A}(x)>0\big\}\cup\mathtt{R}.

For each angle
{\bar{\phi}}\geq{\phi_{0}}
there is only one intersection of
\zeta(s^{-}({\bar{\phi}}))+re^{\imath{\bar{\phi}}}
with the above optimality curve
\mathcal{R}
(being
\mathtt{R}
transversal to
\mathbf{t}
by the above proposition). If the intersection occurs along the level set, then the optimal closing curve is the arc of the level set; if it occurs along the curve
\mathtt{R}
in the point
\xi(a)+b\xi^{\prime}(a)
, then the optimal closing curve is the arc
[\zeta(\phi_{0}),\xi(a)]
of level set plus the segment
[\xi(a),\xi(a)+b\xi^{\prime}(a)]
.

Case 3:

in this case the admissible part of the curve
\mathtt{R}
ends in the final admissibile point
x_{\mathrm{sl}}
, where its tangent makes an angle
\alpha
with the fire direction
\mathbf{t}
.

Define the optimality curve by

\mathcal{R}=\big\{x:u(x)=u(\zeta({\phi_{0}})),\mathcal{A}(x)>0\big\}\cup\big\{x\in\mathtt{R},\mathcal{A}(x)\geq 0\big\}\cup\big\{\text{saturated spiral starting from $x_{\mathrm{sl}}$}\big\}.

For each angle
{\bar{\phi}}\geq{\phi_{0}}
, if the intersection of
\zeta(s^{-}({\bar{\phi}}))+re^{\imath{\bar{\phi}}}
with
\mathcal{R}
occurs on the arc of the saturated spiral starting from
x_{\mathrm{sl}}
, then the solution is the optimal solution as in Case 2 above up to
x_{\mathrm{sl}}
(i.e. an arc of the level set plus a segment ending in
x_{\mathrm{sl}}
), and then the arc of saturated spiral ending in the angle
{\bar{\phi}}
. The other cases (i.e. when the intersection occurs before
\bar{x}
) are treated as in the previous point.

Figure 22
.
The three cases of Theorem
4.12
: in the first picture all level set
\xi
is admissible, and then for every angle
\geq{\phi_{0}}
the optimal solution is an arc of the level set; in the second case all curve
\mathtt{R}
is admissible, and then the optimal solutions for points on
\mathtt{R}
is an arc of
\xi
and a segment; in the third case for large angles the minimal point lies on a saturated spiral, and the optimal solution is an arc of
\xi
, a segment and an arc of saturated spiral.

Theorem 4.12
.

The curves constructed in the three cases above are the unique solution to the minimum problem considered in Theorem
4.6
for
{\bar{\phi}}\in[{\phi_{0}},{\phi_{0}}+2\pi+\beta^{-}({\phi_{0}}))
.

Proof.

The proof is based on a contradiction: if an admissible spiral crosses the optimal solution given in the above Cases 1,2,3, then it is not admissible or not convex (Figure
22
).

Fix an angle
{\bar{\phi}}
, and let
\hat{\zeta}(\phi)=\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+\hat{r}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}
,
\phi\in[{\phi_{0}},{\bar{\phi}}]
be the optimal solution candidate constructed as in Cases 1,2,3 above. Let
\check{\zeta}(\phi)=\zeta(s^{-}(\phi))+\check{r}(\phi)e^{\imath\phi}
be another admissible spiral solution, and
\check{\phi}
be the last angle for which
\hat{r}(\phi)\leq\check{r}(\phi)
for
\phi\in[{\phi_{0}},\check{\phi}]
.

The convexity assumption of
\hat{\zeta}
yields immediately that

L(\hat{\zeta}([{\bar{\phi}},\check{\phi}]))\leq L(\check{\zeta}([{\bar{\phi}},\check{\phi}])),

and then this crossing cannot occur in the saturated part of
\hat{\zeta}
: indeed in this part
\beta(\phi)\leq\alpha
is forced. By the monotonicity assumption on
u\circ\zeta
, the only part where
\check{\zeta}
can cross
\hat{\zeta}
is along the segment part of
\hat{\zeta}
.

The second observation is that
\check{\zeta}
cannot cross
\mathtt{R}
in the admissible region: indeed, it is fairly easy to see that the shortest path from
\zeta({\phi_{0}})
to a point in
\mathtt{R}
outside
\{u\leq u(\zeta({\phi_{0}}))\}
is the solution constructed in Case 2 above. Hence after the crossing the spiral
\check{\zeta}
would not be admissible by Proposition
4.11
.

Hence, the only situation to be studied is the following: the spiral
\check{\zeta}
crosses the segment
(\xi(a),x_{\mathrm{sl}}=\xi(a)+b\xi^{\prime}(a))
in some point
\check{x}
. Considering the region delimited by
\{u=u(\zeta(\phi_{0}))\}\cup\mathcal{R}\cup[\xi(a),x_{\mathrm{sl}}=\xi(a)+b\xi^{\prime}(a)]
, by convexity of
\hat{\zeta}
and the above observation the curve
\check{\zeta}
can only exit along the segment
(\xi(a),x_{\mathrm{sl}})
. This contradicts the convexity of
\check{\zeta}
. ∎

Remark 4.13
.

The argument of the above proof is that the shortest admissible convex curve for
\phi\in[{\phi_{0}},{\phi_{0}}+2\pi+\beta^{-}({\phi_{0}}))
is the one constructed in Cases 1,2,3. Since it is possible to prove that, in the range of angles considered here, the optimal closing barrier is convex, then the above statement is valid for generic simple curves such that
u
is monotone along the curve.

Remark 4.14
.

It is important to notice that the time function
u
used to compute the barrier is fixed, i.e. it does not depend on the barrier we are constructing after
\phi_{0}
: indeed the choice of
\phi\in[{\phi_{0}},{\phi_{0}}+2\pi+\beta^{-}({\phi_{0}}))
gives that we are only using the optimal rays leaving the spiral
\zeta([0,{\phi_{0}}])
to construct the next arc of the spiral, and thus the rays are assigned. When we go above this angle, i.e. after one single round, then the choice of
\zeta
at the first round impacts the behavior of
u
in the next. We will indeed see that it is better to make
\zeta
longer at the previous round in order to have more time at the next. This is easily explained by observing that if we increase the length of
\zeta
by
L
we gain a time
L
, i.e. we can construct a barrier with length
\sigma L>L
at the next round.

5.
A case study: the fastest saturated spiral

In this section we consider as initial data for the saturated spiral the solution considered in Theorem
4.12
, namely an arc of the level set of
u
, a segment and the saturated spiral, where the initial fire set is the ball
\lx@glossaries@newentry{main}{att}{name,description,symbol,symbolplural,text,plural,first,firstplural,sort,counter,see,parent,prefix,short,shortplural,long,longplural}\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate B_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{att}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{a}}}}(0)
,
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{att}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{a}}}\in(0,1)
. The case
\mathtt{a}=1
is the previous case of Section
3.1
.

The aim of this analysis is twofold: on the one hand it is not clear if with this ”optimized” initial data the spiral will close after some time, on the other hand the solution constructed here will be the fundamental block for the analysis of the general case. Since the spiral we construct is the spiral which becomes saturated before any other spiral, we call it the
fastest saturated spiral
. We are not asking the question of its optimality here.

The construction speed we consider in this situation is the critical speed
\bar{\sigma}=\frac{1}{\cos(\bar{\alpha})}=2.6144..
(Corollary
3.10
). The results proved here will be valid also for any other
\sigma\leq\bar{\sigma}
: more precisely, in the critical case the spirals diverges exponentially like
\phi e^{\lambda\phi}
, being
\lambda=\ln(\frac{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})})=0.27995...
the only real eigenvalue of the RDE, and above the critical case it diverges like
e^{\lambda_{1}\phi}
, being
\lambda_{1}
the greater real eigenvalue.

According to Cases 1,2,3 above Theorem
4.12
with
\hat{\phi}=0
, the firefighter constructs a spiral
\mathtt{Z}_{\mathtt{a}}
(
\mathtt{a}
being the radius of the initial burning region
B_{\mathtt{a}}(0)
) starting from a point
P_{0}=(1,0)
with the following structure: it is the union of

(1)

a circle (the arc of the level set of
u
, in some cases consisting of a single point),

(2)

a segment of endpoints
P_{1}(\mathtt{a}),P_{2}(\mathtt{a})
tangent to the circle in
P_{1}(\mathtt{a})
such that the point
P_{2}(\mathtt{a})
is saturated,

\mathcal{A}(P_{2}(\mathtt{a}))=u(P_{2}(\mathtt{a}))-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}}L(P_{2}(\mathtt{a}))=0,

where
L(P_{2}(\mathtt{a}))
is the length of the spiral
\tilde{Z}_{\mathtt{a}}
up to the point
P_{2}(\mathtt{a})
,

(3)

a saturated spiral starting in
P_{2}(\mathtt{a})
.

The relevant quantities determining the exact structure of the spiral barrier are the following:

•

the length of the arc of circle

\widearc{P_{0}P_{1}(\mathtt{a})}=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Deltaphia}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}}},

where
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}
is the angle opening (remember that the level set
u
passing by
(1,0)
is
\partial B_{1}(0)
);

•

the length of the segment in the arc case (see below)

|P_{2}(\mathtt{a})-P_{1}(\mathtt{a})|=\cot(\bar{\alpha}),\quad|P_{2}(\mathtt{a})|=\frac{1}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})|},

where we have used that by convexity and admissibility the segment is tangent to the saturated spiral in
P_{2}(\mathtt{a})
, i.e.
\angle(P_{2}(\mathtt{a}),P_{2}(\mathtt{a})-P_{1}(\mathtt{a}))=\bar{\alpha}
;

•

the saturation condition at
P_{2}(\mathtt{a})
, which can be written now as

\displaystyle u(P_{2}(\mathtt{a}))-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}}L(P_{2}(\mathtt{a}))=\left(\frac{1}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-\mathtt{a}\right)-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}}\left(\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}+\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\right)=0.

Using
\bar{\sigma}=\frac{1}{\cos(\bar{\alpha})}
, the last equation becomes

(5.1)

\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}=\bar{\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-\mathtt{a}\right)-\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}=\tan(\bar{\alpha})-\frac{\mathtt{a}}{\cos(\bar{\alpha})}.

Depending on the value of the initial radius
\mathtt{a}
of the fire initial position, we have the following situations (see Fig.
23
).

Arc case,
\mathtt{a}<\sin(\bar{\alpha})
, i.e.
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}>0
:

then the angle at the origin corresponding to the segment
[P_{1},P_{2}]
is
\theta_{\mathtt{a}}=\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}
.

Segment case,
\mathtt{a}\geq\sin(\bar{\alpha})
, i.e.
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}\leq 0
:

then
P_{1}(\mathtt{a})=P_{0}=(1,0)
, and the tangency and saturation conditions for
P_{2}(\mathtt{a})
give

(|P_{2}(\mathtt{a})|-\mathtt{a})-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}}|P_{1}(\mathtt{a})-P_{2}(\mathtt{a})|=\frac{\sin(\theta_{\mathtt{a}}+\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-\mathtt{a}-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}}\frac{\sin(\theta_{\mathtt{a}})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}=0.

Using again
\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}}=\cos(\bar{\alpha})
we obtain

\cos(\theta_{\mathtt{a}})=\mathtt{a},\quad\theta_{\mathtt{a}}\in\bigg[0,\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}\bigg],

because
\mathtt{a}\in[\sin(\bar{\alpha}),1]
.

Figure 23
.
The two cases with
\mathtt{a}\in(0,\sin(\bar{\alpha}))
and
\mathtt{a}\in[\sin(\bar{\alpha}),1]
.

.

The main result of this section is the following

Theorem 5.1
.

For any value of
\mathtt{a}\in(0,1]
, the spiral
\tilde{Z}_{\mathtt{a}}
does not confine the fire: more precisely, the spiral diverges exponentially as

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)\sim 2\phi e^{\bar{c}\phi}\begin{cases}\frac{e^{-\bar{c}(\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha})}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi)}-\frac{e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2})}-e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}})}}{\bar{c}}-\cot(\bar{\alpha})e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}})}&0\leq\mathtt{a}<\sin(\bar{\alpha}),\\
\frac{\sin(\theta_{\mathtt{a}}+\bar{\alpha})e^{-\bar{c}\theta_{\mathtt{a}}}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi)}-\frac{\sin(\theta_{\mathtt{a}})e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\theta_{\mathtt{a}}+\bar{\alpha})}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}&\sin(\bar{\alpha})\leq\mathtt{a}\leq 1,\end{cases}

being
\bar{c}=\ln(\frac{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})})
given by Corollary
3.10
.

The proof of this theorem relies on a careful application of Lemma
3.14
.

Remark 5.2
.

We will call the spiral
\tilde{Z}_{\mathtt{a}}
as the
fastest saturated spiral
: indeed it is the spiral which becomes saturated as fast as possible (i.e. at the smallest angle) and remains saturated from that angle onward. In the next section we consider the same problem when the initial configuration is an arc of a fixed barrier
\bar{\zeta}\llcorner_{\phi\in[0,\phi_{0}]}
. In both cases we will use the math accent
tilde
will denote the fastest saturated spiral.

Figure 24
.
Here
f_{0}
is made by an arc, a segment and a saturated spiral.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We analyze separately the two cases
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}>0
and
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}\leq 0
.

Case
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}>0
.
This case is described in Figure
24
. We remark that from (
5.1
) we find that
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}\in(0,\tan(\bar{\alpha})]
, the largest possible arc-length being

\Delta\phi_{0}=\tan(\bar{\alpha})

corresponding to
\mathtt{a}=0
. We will denote by
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}
the radius of the spiral
\tilde{Z}_{\mathtt{a}}
in the fire coordinates of Theorem
2.11
, when we need to underline the dependence w.r.t.
\mathtt{a}
, otherwise we will use just
\tilde{r}
to lighten the notation.

Step 1.
We compute the first rounds:

•

for
\phi\in[0,\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}})
we have

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=1;

•

for
\phi\in[\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}},\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha})
we have that

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{\cos(\phi-\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}})},

in particular we call

\kappa_{1}(\mathtt{a})=\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}\left(\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}\right)=\frac{1}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}

the final distance for
0
;

•

for
\phi\in[\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha},2\pi)
it holds

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi),

since the spiral is saturated, giving

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\kappa_{1}(\mathtt{a})e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-\frac{\pi}{2}+\bar{\alpha}-\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}})};

•

the radius
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}
has a jump at
\phi=2\pi
,

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(2\pi)-\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(2\pi-)=-1,

in particular we denote by

\kappa_{2}(\mathtt{a})=\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(2\pi)=\kappa_{1}(\mathtt{a})e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(2\pi-\frac{\pi}{2}+\bar{\alpha}-\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}})}-1;

•

for
\phi\in[2\pi,2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2})
it holds

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi),

therefore

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\kappa_{2}(\mathtt{a})e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-2\pi)}=\left[\kappa_{1}(\mathtt{a})e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(2\pi-\frac{\pi}{2}+\bar{\alpha}-\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}})}-1\right]e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-2\pi)};

•

define

\kappa_{3}(\mathtt{a})=\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}\left(2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\kappa_{2}(\mathtt{a})e^{\frac{\pi}{2}\cot(\bar{\alpha})};

•

for
\phi\in\left[2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2},2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}\right]
the radius
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)
satisfies

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)-1,

whose solution is

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=k_{3}(\mathtt{a})e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-2\pi-\frac{\pi}{2})}+\tan(\bar{\alpha})\big(1-e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-2\pi-\frac{\pi}{2})}\big);

•

the radius
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}
has a jump in
\phi=2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}
:

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}\left(2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}\right)-\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}\left(2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}-\right)=-\cot(\bar{\alpha}),

and also here we will introduce the quantity

\kappa_{4}(\mathtt{a})=\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}\left(2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}\right)=e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}}\kappa_{3}(\mathtt{a})+\tan(\bar{\alpha})\left(1-e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}}\right)-\cot(\bar{\alpha});

•

finally, for
\phi\geq 2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}+\Delta\phi
the function solves

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)-\frac{\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}.

Using the kernel
G(\phi),M(\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
of Remark
3.21
, the solution can be written for
\phi\geq\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}
as

(5.2)

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}G(\phi-\bar{\phi}_{0}(\mathtt{a}))-G(\phi-2\pi)-M(\phi,\bar{\phi}_{1}(\mathtt{a}),\bar{\phi}_{2}(\mathtt{a}))-\cot(\bar{\alpha})G(\phi-\phi_{0}(\mathtt{a})-2\pi-\bar{\alpha}),

where the angles are given by

\bar{\phi}_{0}(\mathtt{a})=\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha},\quad\bar{\phi}_{1}(\mathtt{a})=2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2},\quad\bar{\phi}_{2}(\mathtt{a})=\bar{\phi}_{0}(\mathtt{a})+2\pi+\bar{\alpha}=\Delta\phi_{a}+2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}.

We can translate the solution by
\varphi=\phi-\bar{\phi}_{0}(\mathtt{a})
obtaining

(5.3)

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}G(\varphi)-G\bigg(\varphi+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\varphi}_{1}(\mathtt{a})\bigg)-M(\varphi,\bar{\varphi}_{1}(\mathtt{a}),2\pi+\bar{\alpha})-\cot(\bar{\alpha})G(\varphi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha}),

with

\bar{\varphi}_{1}(\mathtt{a})=2\pi+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\varphi}_{0}=2\pi+\bar{\alpha}-\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}.

Step 2.

The next step is to optimize the solution w.r.t.
\mathtt{a}
: this is done by taking the derivative w.r.t.
\mathtt{a}
of the solution and using the linearity of the Delay ODE. We use the relations (
3.22
), (
3.23
) and

\frac{d}{d\mathtt{a}}\bar{\varphi}_{1}=\frac{1}{\cos(\bar{\alpha})}\quad\text{because of (\ref{eq:Delta:phi:a})},

we obtain that

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathtt{a}}\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\cos(\bar{\alpha})}\bigg[-\mathfrak{d}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}+\bar{\varphi}_{1}}-\cot(\bar{\alpha})G\bigg(\varphi+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\varphi}_{1}\bigg)+\frac{G\big(\varphi+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\varphi}_{1}-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})\big)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}+G(\varphi-\bar{\varphi}_{1})\bigg].

We have thus to study the function

\check{r}(\psi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})G(\psi)-\frac{G(\psi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-G\bigg(\psi-\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg),\quad\psi=\varphi+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\varphi}_{1},

which is the solution to the RDE for the saturated spiral

\frac{d}{d\psi}\check{r}(\psi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\check{r}(\psi)-\frac{\check{r}(\psi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}+\cot(\bar{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}_{0}-\mathfrak{d}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}-\frac{\mathfrak{d}_{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}.

In terms the function
\check{\rho}(\tau)=\check{r}((2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau)e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau}
the RDE becomes

(5.4)

\check{\rho}(\tau)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})g(\tau)-\frac{e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})}g(\tau-1)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-e^{-\bar{c}\frac{\pi}{2}}g\bigg(\tau-\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}\bigg),

which is the solution to the RDE for the saturated spiral

\frac{d}{d\psi}\check{\rho}(\tau)=\check{\rho}(\tau)-\check{\rho}(\tau-1)+\cot(\bar{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}_{0}-e^{-\bar{c}\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathfrak{d}_{\frac{\pi}{2(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})}}-\frac{e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})}\mathfrak{d}_{1}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}.

Figure 25
.
Plot of the function (
5.4
) for
\tau\in[1,2]
on the left and of the first two rounds of the function
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)
(Equation (
5.2
)) multiplied by
e^{-\bar{c}\phi}
.

A numerical plot of the solution for
\tau\in[1,2]
is in Fig.
25
: we notice that it is strictly negative together with its derivative, and from Lemma
3.14
we deduce that
\check{\rho}<0
for all
\tau\geq 1
, corresponding to the angle

\phi=4\pi+\bar{\alpha},\quad\text{because of the map}\ \tau\mapsto\phi=(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau+2\pi.

For the first two rounds, a numerical plot of
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)
as function for
(\phi,\Delta\phi)\in[\Delta\phi+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}]\times[0,\tan(\bar{\alpha})]
shows that the solution is strictly positive (and actually decreasing w.r.t.
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}
, i.e. increasing w.r.t.
\mathtt{a}
), see Fig.
25
.

Step 3.
From now on we will assume
\mathtt{a}=0
, therefore
\Delta\phi_{0}=\tan(\bar{\alpha})
: a numerical plot of the function
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)e^{-\bar{c}\phi}
is in Fig.
25
. We observe that the function is strictly positive and increasing in an interval larger than
2\pi+\bar{\alpha}
, and then by Lemma
3.14
we deduce that the solution is positive for all
\phi
.

Case
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}\leq 0
.
This case is described in Figure
26
. We will prove that the worst case is for
\mathtt{a}=\sin(\bar{\alpha})
, which corresponds to
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}=0
in the previous analysis: hence again the worst case is for
\mathtt{a}=0
, as shown above, and the spiral does not close.

The approach is completely similar, the only variation being the missing initial arc.

Step 1.
We compute
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)
explicitly for the first rounds. We call
\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}=\theta_{\mathtt{a}}+\bar{\alpha}\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
as the slope of the segment
[P_{1}(\mathtt{a}),P_{2}(\mathtt{a})]
:

Figure 26
.
Segment case for the fastest saturated spiral.

•

for
\phi\in[0,\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha}]
we have that

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\frac{\sin(\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}})}{\sin(\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-\phi)},

and we call

\kappa^{\prime}_{1}(\mathtt{a})=\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha})=\frac{\sin(\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})};

•

for
\phi\in[\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha},2\pi)
it holds

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi),

which gives

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\kappa^{\prime}_{1}(\mathtt{a})e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}+\bar{\alpha})};

•

the function
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)
has a jump at
\phi=2\pi
,

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(2\pi)-\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(2\pi-)=-1,

and we set

\kappa^{\prime}_{2}(\mathtt{a})=\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(2\pi)=\frac{\sin(\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(2\pi-\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}+\bar{\alpha})}-1;

•

for
\phi\in[2\pi,2\pi+\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}})
it holds

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi),

so that

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\kappa^{\prime}_{2}(\mathtt{a})e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-2\pi)};

•

in
\phi=2\pi+\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}
the function
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}
has a jump, computed as

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(2\pi+\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}})-\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(2\pi+\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-)=-\frac{\sin(\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})},

and we call

\kappa_{3}^{\prime}(\mathtt{a})=\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(2\pi+\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}})=\kappa^{\prime}_{2}(\mathtt{a})e^{\cot(\bar{\alpha})\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}}-\frac{\sin(\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})};

•

finally, for
\phi\geq 2\pi+\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}
the function solves

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)-\frac{\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}.

As before, by using the kernel
G(\phi)
of Remark
3.21
, the solution can be written for
\phi\geq\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha}
as

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\frac{\sin(\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}G(\phi-\bar{\phi}_{0}(\mathtt{a}))-G(\phi-2\pi)-\frac{\sin(\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}G(\phi-\bar{\phi}_{0}(\mathtt{a})-2\pi-\bar{\alpha}),

where the angle
\bar{\phi}_{0}(\mathtt{a})
given by

\bar{\phi}_{0}(\mathtt{a})=\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha}.

Step 2.
Taking the derivative w.r.t.
\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}
and using (
3.22
) we get

\displaystyle\check{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}}\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\cos(\theta_{\mathtt{a}})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-\frac{\sin(\theta_{\mathtt{a}})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\cot(\bar{\alpha})\bigg)G(\phi-\phi_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\frac{\sin(\theta_{\mathtt{a}})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}-\frac{\cos(\theta_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}+\frac{\sin(\theta_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\cot(\bar{\alpha})\bigg)G(\phi-\phi_{0}-(2\pi+\bar{\alpha}))

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\sin(\theta_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}G(\phi-\phi_{0}-2(2\pi+\bar{\alpha}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sin(\theta_{\mathtt{a}}-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}\Big(G(\phi-\phi_{0})-2\cos(\bar{\alpha})G(\phi-\phi_{0}-(2\pi+\bar{\alpha}))+G(\phi-\phi_{0}-2(2\pi+\bar{\alpha}))\Big).

The latter function, when rescaled by
\rho(\tau)=\check{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi_{0}+(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau)e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau}
is proportional to

(5.5)

g(\tau)-2\cos(\bar{\alpha})e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})}g(\tau-1)+e^{-2\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})}g(\tau-2),

The numerical plot (Fig.
27
) shows that it is positive and strictly increasing, hence by Lemma
3.14
we conclude that
\frac{d\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}}{d\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}}<0
, so the worst case is
\bar{\theta}_{\mathtt{a}}=\frac{\pi}{2}
, which corresponds to the arc case when
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}=0
.

Figure 27
.
Plot of the function (
5.5
).

Asymptotic behavior.
For
\mathtt{a}\in[0,\sin(\bar{\alpha})]
the solution is given by (
5.2
):

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}G(\phi-\bar{\phi}_{0}(\mathtt{a}))-G(\phi-2\pi)-M(\phi,\bar{\phi}_{1}(\mathtt{a}),\bar{\phi}_{2}(\mathtt{a}))-\cot(\bar{\alpha})G(\phi-\phi_{0}(\mathtt{a})-2\pi-\bar{\alpha}).

Using the asymptotic expansions of Corollary
3.23
, we obtain that the coefficient in front of the principal term
\phi e^{\bar{c}\phi}
is

\displaystyle\frac{2e^{-\bar{c}\bar{\phi}_{0}}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-2e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi)}-\int_{\bar{\phi}_{1}}^{\bar{\phi}_{2}}2e^{-\bar{c}\phi_{0}}d\phi_{0}-2\cot(\bar{\alpha})e^{-\bar{c}\bar{\phi}_{2}},

which is the first case of the asymptotic expansion. The other case is completely similar.
∎

5.1.
An estimate on the length of the fastest saturated spiral

We conclude this section with an estimate on the length
L
of the fastest saturated spiral when
\mathtt{a}=0
. Writing the solution (
5.3
) with
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}=\tan(\bar{\alpha})
in the variable

\rho(\tau)=\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi_{0}+(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau)e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau},\quad\phi_{0}(0)=\tan(\bar{\alpha})+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha},

we obtain

(5.6)

\rho(\tau)=\frac{1}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}g(\tau)-e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha}-\frac{\pi}{2}-\tan(\bar{\alpha}))}g(\tau-\tau_{1})-m(\tau,\tau_{2},1)-\cot(\bar{\alpha})e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})}g(\tau-1),

where

\tau_{1}=1-\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}+\tan(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}},\quad\tau_{2}=1-\frac{\tan(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}.

Using the definitions of
\mathfrak{g}(\tau)
and
\mathfrak{G}(\tau)
and that

\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}=\tan(\bar{\alpha})+\cot(\bar{\alpha})+\int_{\phi_{0}}^{\phi}\frac{\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi^{\prime})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}d\phi^{\prime},\quad\phi\geq\phi_{0},

we thus have that the length of the spiral is

(5.7)

\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\tau)=\begin{cases}0&\tau<-\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}+\tan(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}},\\
(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}+\tan(\bar{\alpha})&-\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}+\tan(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}\leq\tau<-\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}},\\
\tan(\bar{\alpha})+\cot(\bar{\alpha}-(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau)&-\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}\leq\tau<0,\\
\begin{aligned} &\tan(\bar{\alpha})+\cot(\bar{\alpha})+\frac{\mathfrak{g}(\tau)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-\mathfrak{g}(\tau-\tau_{1})\\
&\quad-(\mathfrak{G}(\tau-\tau_{2})-\mathfrak{G}(\tau-1))-\cot(\bar{\alpha})\mathfrak{g}(\tau-1)\end{aligned}&\tau\geq 0.\end{cases}

The first terms take into account the initial arc and the initial segment.

Proposition 5.3
.

It holds for
\phi\geq\tan(\bar{\alpha})+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}

\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)-2.08\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})\geq 0.67e^{\bar{c}(\phi-\tan(\bar{\alpha})-\frac{\pi}{2}+\bar{\alpha})}.

Figure 28
.
Numerical computations of the function (
5.8
), with a blow-up about the minimum.

Proof.

We numerically compute the function

(5.8)

\tau\mapsto\rho(\tau)-2.08e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau}\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\tau-1),

which is the rescaling of the function in the statement: Fig.
28
shows that the function has a minimum
>0.67
for
\tau\in[3,4]
and it is increasing for
\tau\in[4,5]
.

In order to apply Lemma
3.14
, we write the equation for
r-2.08L
. By using the equations for
\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{G}
, Equations (
3.24
) and (
3.25
), we have that the
\phi
-derivative of
L(\phi)
for
\phi\geq\phi_{0}
is

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)-\frac{\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}

\displaystyle\quad+\big(\tan(\bar{\alpha})+\cot(\bar{\alpha})\big)\delta_{\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}+\tan(\bar{\alpha})}+\frac{H(\phi-\tan(\bar{\alpha})-\frac{\pi}{2}+\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-H(\phi-2\pi)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\max\{0,\phi-\frac{5\pi}{2}\}-\max\{0,\phi-\frac{5\pi}{2}-\tan(\bar{\alpha})\}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-\cot(\bar{\alpha})H\bigg(\phi-\frac{5\pi}{2}-\tan(\bar{\alpha})\bigg).

Recall that
H(\phi)
is the Heaviside function. Hence for
\phi\geq\frac{5\pi}{2}+\tan(\bar{\alpha})
we have

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)-\frac{\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}+\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{SL}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{L}}},

where

(5.9)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{SL}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{L}}}=\frac{1}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-1-\tan(\bar{\alpha})-\cot(\bar{\alpha})=-2.7473<0.

Hence for
\phi\geq\tan(\bar{\alpha})+\frac{\pi}{2}-\bar{\alpha}+2(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\phi}(\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)-2.08\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha}))

\displaystyle=\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)-2.08\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha}))

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})-2.08\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2(2\pi+\bar{\alpha}))}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-S_{L}

\displaystyle\geq\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)-2.08\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha}))

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})-2.08\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2(2\pi+\bar{\alpha}))}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}.

Hence in terms of
\tau
we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\tau}(\rho(\tau)-2.08e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau}\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\tau-1))&\geq(\rho(\tau)-2.08e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau}\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\tau-1))\\
&\quad-(\rho(\tau-1)-2.08e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})(\tau-1)}\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\tau-2)),\end{split}

so that Lemma
3.14
and Remark
3.15
can be applied.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

The asymptotic constant for the length should be

(5.10)

\frac{\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)}{\tilde{L}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}\sim e^{\bar{c}}\bar{c}\sin(\bar{\alpha})=2.08884,

obtained by using
\tilde{r}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(\phi)\sim\phi e^{\bar{c}\phi}
. However for this exact value we cannot use Lemma
3.14
, obviously.

6.
A differentiable path of spirals

Let
\bar{\zeta}(s)
,
s\in[0,L]
with
L\gg 1
, be a given admissible spiral. In this section we construct a family of admissible spirals
\tilde{\zeta}(s;s_{0})
,
s\in[0,L]
, with the following properties:

(1)

for
s\in[0,s_{0}]
it holds
\tilde{\zeta}(s;s_{0})=\bar{\zeta}(s)
;

(2)

in the first round starting from
\bar{\zeta}(s_{0})
, the spiral
\tilde{\zeta}(s;s_{0})
is the spiral given by Theorem
4.12
;

(3)

after the first round, the spiral
\tilde{\zeta}(s;s_{0})
is the saturated spiral.

We call each spiral
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Ztildes0}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tilde{Z}(s_{0})}}=\tilde{\zeta}([0,L];s_{0})
the
fastest saturated spiral starting from
s_{0}
. The map
s_{0}\mapsto\tilde{Z}(s_{0})
defines a path in the space of admissible spirals, connecting the fastest saturated spiral of the previous section (corresponding to
\tilde{\zeta}(s;0)=\tilde{\zeta}_{\mathtt{a}=0}(s)
) with the given spiral
\bar{Z}=\bar{\zeta}([0,s])
.

If we denote by
\phi_{0}=(s^{+})^{-1}(s_{0})
the starting angle of the above construction, then the first round is determined by

\phi_{0}\leq\phi\leq\phi_{0}+\beta^{-}(\phi_{0}),

In the following we will write both
\tilde{\zeta}(\cdot;s_{0}),\tilde{r}(\cdot;s_{0})
and
\tilde{\zeta}(\cdot;\phi_{0}),\tilde{r}(\cdot;\phi_{0})
to denote this admissible family of spirals, and the argument
\cdot
can be either the arc length
s
or the rotation angle
\phi
.

It is immediate to deduce the following lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

The family of spirals
\tilde{\zeta}(s;s_{0})
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

\tilde{\zeta}(\cdot;L)=\bar{\zeta}(\cdot)
;

(2)

\tilde{\zeta}(s;0)
is the fastest saturated spiral constructed in Section
5
;

(3)

if
s
belongs to the first round starting from
\bar{\zeta}(s_{0})
, then the solution is given by Theorem
4.12
;

(4)

after the first round, denoting with

\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})
the angle-ray representation, we have

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})-\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi-2\pi+\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}.

Since the construction of Theorem
4.12
is explicit, the last point above yields that the family is completely determined, up to computing the solution in the first round after
s_{0}
and solving the saturated RDE.

The main result of this section is to prove that this family is
C^{1}
-w.r.t. the parameter
s_{0}
: more precisely, we will show that the limit of the incremental ratio

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{deltartildefam}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})}}:=\lim_{\delta s_{0}\searrow 0}\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0}+\delta s_{0})-\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})}{\delta s_{0}}

exists outside a finite set of angles
\phi
, and it satisfies a precise RDE, which allows the explicit computation of the
\delta\tilde{r}
. The dependence on the base spiral
\bar{\zeta}
appears only in two real parameters describing the initial data of
\delta\tilde{r}
, allowing to write its explicit form with computations similar to Section
5
: more precisely, the form of the RDE satisfied by the perturbation is very similar to the RDE satisfied by the solution
r(\phi)
or its derivative
\frac{d}{d\mathtt{a}}r(\phi)
considered in Section
5
, and will be studied with similar techniques. In particular, depending whether the non-saturated part of the fastest closing spiral at the first round is a segment or is an arc+segment, the derivative takes different form, and we will study them separately in two different subsections, Subsections
6.1
and
6.2
: these derivatives coincide at the transition point between segment and arc.

Remark 6.2
.

We remark that the segment case can be recovered from the arc case when the initial level set is just one point, see Remark
6.12
: however we believe that it is simpler and very instructive to perform the analysis in the segment case, so that we present it in the next subsection.

We anticipate also that out that
\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})
is not positive in an initial interval of the second round, and thus it cannot be optimal for some angles. Hence to prove Theorem
1.12
we will need an additional perturbation of the last round: we address this fact in Section
8
.

Since in the last round we have already the explicit form of the optimal closing solution (Theorem
4.12
), we can assume that
\zeta
makes at least one round after
\phi_{0}
, so that there will always be a saturated part.

6.1.
Analysis of
\tilde{\zeta}(s;s_{0})
- Segment Case

The assumption on
\tilde{\zeta}(\phi;s_{0})
is that for
\phi\in\phi_{0}+[0,2\pi+\beta^{-}(\phi_{0}))
it is made of

•

a segment
[P_{1},P_{2}]
where
P_{1}=\bar{\zeta}(s_{0})=\bar{\zeta}(s^{+}_{0}(\phi_{0}))
, followed by

•

a saturated spiral with
\beta(\phi)=\bar{\alpha}
.

The spiral
\tilde{r}(\phi,s_{0})
will then proceed for all angles
\phi\geq\phi_{0}+2\pi+\beta^{-}(\phi_{0})
as a saturated spiral, i.e.
\beta(\phi)=\bar{\alpha}
constant. Following the notation of Step 1 in Case
\Delta\phi_{\mathtt{a}}=0
of the proof of Theorem
5.1
, we will denote by
\bar{\theta}
the angle

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{thetatildeseg}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{\theta}}}=\angle(e^{i\phi_{0}},[P_{1},P_{2}])\in\bigg[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg].

The case
\bar{\theta}=\frac{\pi}{2}
corresponds to the transition between the segment-case and the arc-case.

Due to uniqueness of the construction, the same structure (i.e. an initial segment followed by a saturated spiral) holds for the spiral
\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0}+\delta s_{0})
, starting from the point
\bar{\zeta}(s_{0}+\delta s_{0})
if
\delta s_{0}\ll 1
and
\bar{\theta}<\frac{\pi}{2}
, i.e. there will be an initial segment

[P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2}^{\prime}]=[P_{1}+\delta P_{1},P_{2}+\delta P_{2}],

with the angles

s^{+}(\phi_{0}^{\prime})=s_{0}^{+}(\phi_{0}+\delta\phi_{0})=s_{0}+\delta s_{0},\quad\bar{\theta}^{\prime}=\bar{\theta}+\delta\bar{\theta}=\angle(e^{i\phi_{0}},[P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2}^{\prime}]),

followed by a saturated spiral. The analysis of this section refers to Figure
29
.

Figure 29
.
Analysis of perturbations for the segment case.

Since the spirals are convex and there are no smaller admissible convex spirals for the first round (
\phi\in[\phi_{0},\phi_{0}+2\pi+\beta^{-}(\phi_{0}))
), by the convexity of the level sets of
u
one deduces that

0<\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{theta0pertsegm}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\theta_{0}=\beta^{+}(s_{0})}}\leq\bar{\theta},\quad\bar{\alpha}\leq\bar{\theta},

where the second is due to the fact that the point
\bar{\zeta}(s_{0})
is not saturated if
P_{1}\not=P_{2}
. We will denote by
\theta
the angle

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{thetapertsegm}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\theta}}=\bar{\theta}-\theta_{0}.

We compute now the variations up to second order terms w.r.t.
\delta s_{0}
:

\delta P_{1}=\delta s_{0}e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\theta_{0})},\quad\delta\phi_{0}=\frac{\delta s_{0}\sin(\theta_{0})}{r(\phi_{0})},

\displaystyle\delta P_{2}

\displaystyle=\delta P_{1}+\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta})}+|P_{2}-P_{1}|\delta\bar{\theta}e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}+\frac{\pi}{2})}

\displaystyle=\delta s_{0}e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\theta_{0})}+\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta})}+|P_{2}-P_{1}|\delta\bar{\theta}e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}+\frac{\pi}{2})}.

Since the point
P_{2}^{\prime}
is saturated, we obtain

0=u(P_{2}^{\prime})-\cos(\bar{\alpha})(\delta s_{0}+|P_{2}^{\prime}-P_{1}^{\prime}|+L(P_{0})),

where
L(P_{0})
is the length of the spiral up to the angle
\phi_{0}
. In particular, observing that
\nabla u(P_{2})=e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha})}
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha})}\cdot\delta P_{2}-\cos(\bar{\alpha})(\delta s_{0}+\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|)

\displaystyle=\delta s_{0}\cos(\bar{\theta}-\theta_{0}-\bar{\alpha})+\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|\cos(\bar{\alpha})-|P_{2}-P_{1}|\delta\theta\sin(\bar{\alpha})-\cos(\bar{\alpha})(\delta s_{0}+\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|)

\displaystyle=\delta s_{0}\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)+\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|\cos(\bar{\alpha})-|P_{2}-P_{1}|\delta\theta\sin(\bar{\alpha})-\cos(\bar{\alpha})(\delta s_{0}+\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|),

where we recall that
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{w}
denotes the scalar product of
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We thus deduce that

(6.1)

\frac{|P_{2}-P_{1}|\delta\theta}{\delta s_{0}}=\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}.

Imposing that the angle at
P_{2}^{\prime}
with the optimal ray is equal to the critical angle
\bar{\alpha}
, we deduce that
P_{2}^{\prime}
corresponds to the angle
\phi_{2}+\delta\theta
, where
\phi_{2}=\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}
is the angle corresponding to
P_{2}
in the angle-ray representation for
\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})
. Hence, imposing this relation we obtain

\displaystyle r(\phi_{2})\delta\theta

\displaystyle=e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}+\frac{\pi}{2})}\cdot\delta P_{2}

\displaystyle=\delta s_{0}\sin(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)+\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|\sin(\bar{\alpha})+|P_{2}-P_{1}|\delta\theta\cos(\bar{\alpha}),

so that using (
6.1
) we conclude that

(6.2)

\begin{split}\frac{\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|}{\delta s_{0}}-\frac{r(\phi_{2})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\frac{\delta\theta}{\delta s_{0}}&=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha})-\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}+\frac{\sin(\theta-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\\
&=\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha})^{2}-\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}\\
&=-1+\frac{1-\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}.\end{split}

To compute the perturbation
\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})
for
\phi\geq\phi_{2}
, we observe that
\beta(\phi)=\bar{\alpha}
in this region, so that, denoting with
R(\phi)
the radius of curvature of the base spiral
r(\phi)
, one obtains

Lemma 6.3
.

The RDE satisfied by
\tilde{r}
for
\phi\geq\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}
is

(6.3)

\frac{d}{d\phi}\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})-\begin{cases}R(\phi)&\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}\leq\phi<\phi_{0}+2\pi+\beta^{-}(\phi_{0}),\\
0&\phi_{0}+2\pi+\beta^{-}(\phi_{0})\leq\phi<\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta},\\
|P_{2}-P_{1}|\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}}+\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}&\phi\geq\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We have just to compute the radius of curvature, since
\beta=\bar{\alpha}
fixed. Formula (
6.3
) follows easily, observing that if
\beta^{-}(s_{0})<\bar{\theta}
then there is corner in the spiral
\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})
whose subdifferential is

\partial^{-}\tilde{r}(\phi_{0};s_{0})=\big\{e^{i\phi},\phi\in\phi_{0}+[\beta^{-}(\phi_{0}),\bar{\theta}]\big\}.

The jump at
\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}
is due to the segment
[P_{1},P_{2}]
, corresponding in a jump in
s^{-}(\phi)
at that angle.
∎

In the next proposition we compute the RDE satisfied by the derivative
\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})
w.r.t.
s_{0}
, for angles
\phi>\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

For
\phi>\phi_{2}=\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}
, the derivative

\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})=\lim_{\delta s_{0}\searrow 0}\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0}+\delta s_{0})-\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})}{\delta s_{0}}

satisfies the RDE on
\mathbb{R}

\frac{d}{d\phi}\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})-\frac{\delta\tilde{r}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}+S(\phi;s_{0}),

with source

\displaystyle S(\phi;s_{0})

\displaystyle=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{1-\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}}-\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{0}+2\pi+\theta_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\cos(\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}}+\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}},

where
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
is the distributional derivative of the Dirac’s delta
\mathfrak{d}
.

Proof.

We compute first the variation of the initial data of (
6.3
): observing that the spiral
\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})
is saturated at
\phi_{2}
and hence its tangent vector is
e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta})}
, one has up to second order terms for
\phi
close to
\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0}+\delta s_{0})-\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\imath(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\frac{\pi}{2})}\cdot\delta P_{2}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}

\displaystyle=\delta s_{0}\frac{\sin(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-\frac{|P_{2}-P_{1}|\delta\theta}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}

\displaystyle=\delta s_{0}\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{1-\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})},

which gives the first jump at
\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}
.

Being
R(\phi)
the same up to
\phi_{0}+2\pi+\theta_{0}
, the RDE for
\delta\tilde{r}
is actually the ODE

\frac{d}{d\phi}\delta\tilde{r}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\delta\tilde{r}(\phi)

for
\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}\leq\phi<\phi_{0}+2\pi+\theta_{0}
.

Using
R=Ds^{-}
, the variation at
\phi_{0}+2\pi+\theta_{0}
is

\displaystyle\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0}+\delta s_{0})-\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})

\displaystyle=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\big(\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0}+\delta s_{0})-\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})\big)-\big(s^{-}(\phi)-s^{-}(\phi_{0}+2\pi+\theta_{0})\big)

\displaystyle=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\big(\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0}+\delta s_{0})-\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})\big)-\delta s_{0}.

Hence
\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})
has a jump of size
-1
at the angle
\phi_{0}+2\pi+\theta_{0}
. From (
6.3
) it follows that

\frac{d}{d\phi}\delta\tilde{r}(\phi)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\delta\tilde{r}(\phi)

for
\phi_{0}+2\pi+\theta_{0}<\phi<\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}
.

At the angle
\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}
, computing the first order approximations for
0\leq\phi-\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}\ll 1
one gets up to second order terms

\displaystyle\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0}+\delta s_{0})-\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})

\displaystyle=\bigg[\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta};s_{0}+\delta s_{0})+\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta};s_{0}+\delta s_{0})(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta})

\displaystyle\quad\quad-|P_{2}^{\prime}-P_{1}^{\prime}|\big(1+\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta}-\delta\bar{\theta})\big)1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{\phi\geq\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}+\delta\bar{\theta}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0}+\delta s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta}-\delta\bar{\theta})\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad-\bigg[\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+2\pi+\theta-;s_{0})+\cot(\bar{\alpha})\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+2\pi+\theta;s_{0})(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta})

\displaystyle\quad\quad-|P_{2}-P_{1}|\big(1+\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta})\big)1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{\phi\geq\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\big(\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta};s_{0}+\delta s_{0})-\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta};s_{0})\big)\big(1+\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta})\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0}+\delta s_{0})-\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta})

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(-\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|+\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\bigg)1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{\phi\geq\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}}+|P_{2}-P_{1}|1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{[\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta},\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}+\delta\theta)}.

For the above formula, we have used that the jumps
|P_{2}-P_{1}|
are in different angles and that the curvature term
-\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}
is applied starting from different angles.

Passing to the limit
\delta s_{0}\searrow 0
we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})

\displaystyle=\delta\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta};s_{0})\big(1+\cot(\bar{\alpha})(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta})\big)-\frac{\delta\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\bar{\theta})

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(-\frac{\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|}{\delta s_{0}}+\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\frac{\delta\theta}{\delta s_{0}}\bigg)1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{\phi\geq\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}}+|P_{2}-P_{1}|\frac{\delta\theta}{\delta s_{0}}\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}}.

Hence its derivative w.r.t.
\phi
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\phi}\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})

\displaystyle=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})-\frac{\delta\tilde{r}(\phi-2\pi-\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(-\frac{\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|}{\delta s_{0}}+\frac{\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\frac{\delta\theta}{\delta s_{0}}\bigg)\mathfrak{d}_{\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}}+|P_{2}-P_{1}|\frac{\delta\theta}{\delta s_{0}}\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{\phi_{0}+2\pi+\bar{\theta}},

where
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
is the derivative of the Dirac’s delta:

\langle\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{x},f\rangle=-\frac{df(x)}{dx}.

Using (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) we obtain

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta|P_{2}-P_{1}|}{\delta s_{0}}+\frac{\bar{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha};s_{0})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\frac{\delta\theta}{\delta s_{0}}

\displaystyle=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-\frac{\sin(\theta-\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cos(\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}},

|P_{2}-P_{1}|\frac{\delta\theta}{\delta s_{0}}=\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}.

It is now easy to verify that the derivative
\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})\llcorner_{\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}}
satisfies the RDE in the statement, since in the remaining part of
\mathbb{R}
it solves the linear RDE of the saturated spiral.
∎

Since

\sin(\theta)-\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}=\cot(\bar{\alpha})(1-\cos(\theta))\geq 0,

it follows that the perturbation is positive for
\phi\in[\phi_{0},\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}]
, and it is given explicitly by

\delta\tilde{r}(\phi;s_{0})=\frac{1}{\sin(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\phi)}\bigg(\sin(\theta)-\frac{|P(\phi)-P_{1}|}{|P_{2}-P_{1}|}\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\bigg),

where
P(\phi)
is the position of the point on
[P_{1},P_{2}]
corresponding to the angle
\phi\in[\theta_{0},\theta_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}]
.

Corollary 6.5
.

The solution
\delta\tilde{r}(\phi)
for
\phi\geq\phi_{2}=\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}
is explicitly given by

\begin{split}\delta\tilde{r}(\phi)&=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{1-\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}G(\phi-\phi_{0}-\bar{\theta}+\bar{\alpha})-G(\phi-\phi_{0}-2\pi-\theta_{0})\\
&\quad+\bigg(1-\frac{1-\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}+\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\bigg)G(\phi-2\pi-\phi_{0}-\bar{\theta})\\
&\quad+\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\mathfrak{d}_{\phi-2\pi-\phi_{0}-\bar{\theta}}-\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}G(\phi-4\pi-\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}),\end{split}

where
G(\phi)
is the Green kernel for the RDE.

Proof.

The formula follows from Remark
3.21
.
∎

To proceed further, we rewrite the RDE translating
\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}
to
0
and then use the new variable

(6.4)

\delta\rho(\tau)=\delta\tilde{r}(\phi_{0}+\bar{\theta}-\bar{\alpha}+(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau;s_{0})e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\tau}.

Corollary 6.6
.

The values of the perturbation
\delta r(s;s_{0})
depend uniquely on the angle
\theta=\bar{\theta}-\theta_{0}\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
, and the function
\delta\rho(\tau)
of Equation (
6.4
) is given explicitly by the formula

(6.5)

\delta\rho(\tau)=S_{0}g(\tau)+S_{1}g(\tau-\tau_{1})+S_{2}g(\tau-1)+D\mathfrak{d}_{1}+S_{3}g(\tau-2),\quad\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{tau1}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tau_{1}}}=1-\frac{\theta}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}},

with

(6.6)

S_{0}(\theta)=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{1-\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})},

S_{1}(\theta)=-e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha}-\theta)}=-\frac{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}e^{\bar{c}\theta},

\begin{split}S_{2}(\theta)&=\bigg(1-\frac{1-\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}+\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\bigg)e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})}\\
&=\frac{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}-\frac{1-\cos(\theta)}{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\sin(\bar{\alpha})}+\frac{\cot(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}\big(\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})\big),\end{split}

D(\theta)=\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})}=\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}},

S_{3}(\theta)=-\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})^{2}}e^{-\bar{c}(4\pi+2\bar{\alpha})}=-\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})^{2}}.

We remark that we have used Equation (
3.11
) to replace
e^{-\bar{c}(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})}
with
\frac{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}
.

The study of the sign of
\delta\rho
given by (
6.5
) is done in the next proposition, which is the main statement of this section concerning the segment case. For completeness, we let
\theta
to vary in
[0,\pi]
.

Proposition 6.7
.

The solution
\delta\rho(\tau)=\delta\rho(\tau,\theta)
is strictly positive outside the region

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Nsegm}{{{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}N_{\mathrm{segm}}}}:=\bigg\{1-\frac{\theta}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}\leq\tau\leq 1,0\leq\theta\leq\hat{\theta}\bigg\}\cup\big\{\theta=0\}\cup\{\tau=1,\theta\in[2\bar{\alpha},\pi]\big\}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[0,\pi],

where the angle
\hat{\theta}
is determined by the unique solution to

(6.7)

\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{1-\cos(\hat{\theta})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-e^{-\cot(\bar{\alpha})(2\pi+\bar{\alpha}-\hat{\theta})}=0,\qquad\hat{\theta}\in[0,\pi].

Moreover, as
\tau\to\infty
, the function
\delta\rho(\tau)
diverges like

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{\delta\rho(\tau)e^{-\bar{c}\tau}}{\tau}=2(S_{0}+S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}),\quad 2(S_{0}+S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3})\in\theta^{2}(0.08,028).

Figure 30
.
Plot of the function (
6.7
) on the left side, and of the solution (
6.9
) on the right side.

A numerical computation gives the value (see Fig.
30
)

(6.8)

\hat{\theta}=0.506134.

Proof.

We start by observing that for
\theta=0
the coefficients (
6.6
) are all
0
, so that the solution (
6.5
) is
0
. Hence
\{\theta=0\}\subset N_{\mathrm{segm}}
.

Step 1: computation of the first round.

For
\tau\in[0,1)
the solution is explicitly computed as

(6.9)

\delta\rho(\tau)=S_{0}e^{\tau}+S_{1}e^{\tau-\tau_{1}}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{\tau\geq\tau_{1}}=e^{\tau}(S_{0}+S_{1}e^{-\tau_{1}}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{\tau\geq\tau_{1}}).

The solution
\delta\rho
is thus negative only when
\tau_{1}\leq\tau<1
and iff

S_{0}+S_{1}e^{-\tau_{1}}=\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{1-\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-\frac{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}e^{(\bar{c}+\frac{1}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}})\theta-1}\leq 0,

which is (
6.7
). This gives the angle
\hat{\theta}
: indeed
the function

\theta\mapsto f(\theta)=(1-\cos(\theta))e^{-(\bar{c}+\frac{1}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}})\theta}=(1-\cos(\theta))e^{-\cot(\bar{\alpha})\theta}

has first derivative

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\big(\sin(\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})(1-\cos(\theta))\big)e^{-\cot(\bar{\alpha})\theta}=\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}e^{-\cot(\bar{\alpha})\theta}>0\quad\text{if}\ \theta>0,

because
\bar{\alpha}>\frac{\pi}{4}
, and

f(0)=-1,\quad f\bigg(\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg)=e^{-\cot(\bar{\alpha})\frac{\pi}{2}}>0.

Hence there is only one zero for
f
, and numerically one obtains the value
\hat{\theta}=0.506134
, see Figure
30
.

Figure 31
.
Plot of the solution
\delta\rho(\tau;\theta)
and of
\frac{\delta\rho(\tau;\theta)}{\theta^{2}}
in the segment case for
\tau\in[1,2)
. Notice that the discontinuity for
\tau=\tau_{1}
becomes here a discontinuity in the derivative in
1+\tau_{1}
, and this is the reason why
\delta\rho\sim\theta
as
\theta\in[1+\tau_{1},2)
. In the next step we will have
\delta\rho\sim\theta^{2}
in
[2+\tau_{1},3)
.

Step 2: the function
\frac{\delta\rho(\tau,\theta)}{\theta^{2}}
is strictly positive for
\tau\in[1,1+\tau_{1})
and increasing w.r.t.
\tau
.

For
\tau=[1,1+\tau_{1})
it holds

\displaystyle\delta\rho(\tau)

\displaystyle=D\mathfrak{d}_{1}+S_{0}\big(e^{\tau}-e^{\tau-1}(\tau-1)\big)+S_{1}e^{\tau-\tau_{1}}+S_{2}e^{\tau-1}.

Elementary computations show as before that since
\bar{\alpha}>\frac{\pi}{4}

D=\frac{\cos(\bar{\alpha}-\theta)-\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}\leq 0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\theta\in\{0\}\cup[2\bar{\alpha},\pi],

and in the interval
\theta\in[0,\pi]
the only
0
is for
\theta=0
which corresponds to
D=0
.

When
1<\tau<1+\tau_{1}
the positivity of
\delta\rho
depends on

\displaystyle f(\theta)

\displaystyle=S_{0}(e-\tau+1)+S_{1}e^{1-\tau_{1}}+S_{2},

whose worst case is for
\tau=1+\tau_{1}
:

f(\theta)=S_{0}(e-\tau_{1})+S_{1}e^{1-\tau_{1}}+S_{2}.

Near
\bar{\theta}=\theta_{0}
we have the expansion

(6.10)

\begin{split}f(\theta)&=\bigg(\frac{\cot(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}(e-1)-\frac{1}{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\sin(\bar{\alpha})}-2\frac{\cot(\bar{\alpha})\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}\bigg)\frac{(\bar{\theta}-\theta_{0})^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\theta^{3})\\
&\sim 0.582367\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\theta^{3}),\end{split}

so that we consider the function

f^{\prime}(\theta)=\frac{\delta\rho(\tau,\theta)}{\theta^{2}},\quad 0\leq\theta\leq\pi,1\leq\tau\leq 1+\tau_{1}.

A numerical plot shows that this function is increasing w.r.t.
\tau
for all fixed
\theta
, and its minimal value is

f^{\prime}(1,\pi)=0.17959.

Step 3: the function
\frac{\delta\rho(\tau,\theta)}{\theta^{2}}
is strictly positive and increasing for
\tau\in[1+\tau_{1}(\theta),2)
, and diverging near
\theta=0
.

For
\tau\in[1+\tau_{1},2)
the solution is

\displaystyle\delta\rho(\tau)

\displaystyle=S_{0}\big(e^{\tau}-e^{\tau-1}(\tau-1)\big)+S_{1}\big(e^{\tau-\tau_{1}}-e^{\tau-1-\tau_{1}}(\tau-1-\tau_{1})\big)+S_{2}e^{\tau-1}.

Again numerically the function

f(\tau,\theta)=\frac{\delta\rho(\tau,\theta)}{\theta^{2}}

has a strictly positive derivative w.r.t.
\tau
, and it is strictly positive, with minimal value at

f(1+\tau_{1},\pi)=0.226635.

We compute the expansion at
\theta=0
finding

\displaystyle\delta\rho(\tau,\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}\bigg(\tau-2+\frac{\theta}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}\bigg),

so that
f
is diverging as
\frac{\sin(\bar{\alpha})(1-\xi)}{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})^{2}\theta}
as
\theta\to 0
and
\tau=2-\xi\frac{\theta}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}
,
\xi\in[0,1]
.

Figure 32
.
Plot of the function
\delta\rho(\tau;\theta)
(Equation (
6.11
)) on the left side and of
\frac{\delta\rho(\tau;\theta)}{\theta^{2}}
on the right side for
\tau\in[2,3)
.

Step 4: for
\tau\in[2,3]
the function
\delta\rho(\tau,\theta)/\theta^{2}
is bounded and strictly positive.

The solution in this interval is written as

(6.11)

\begin{split}\delta\rho(\tau,\theta)&=S_{0}\bigg(e^{\tau}-e^{\tau-1}(\tau-1)+e^{\tau-2}\frac{(\tau-2)^{2}}{2}\bigg)\\
&\quad+S_{1}\bigg(e^{\tau-\tau_{1}}-e^{\tau-1-\tau_{1}}(\tau-1-\tau_{1})+e^{\tau-2-\tau_{1}}\frac{(\tau-2-\tau_{1})^{2}}{2}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{\tau\geq 2+\tau_{1}}\bigg)\\
&\quad+S_{2}\big(e^{\tau-1}-e^{\tau-2}(\tau-2)\big)+S_{3}e^{\tau-2}.\end{split}

Its expansion at
\theta=0
is quadratic in
\theta
,

\displaystyle\delta\rho(\tau,\theta)

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{\cot(\bar{\alpha})}{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\bigg(e^{\tau}-e^{\tau-1}(\tau-1)+e^{\tau-2}\frac{(\tau-2)^{2}}{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\frac{2\cos(\bar{\alpha})^{2}+1}{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})\sin(\bar{\alpha})}\big(e^{\tau-1}-e^{\tau-2}(\tau-2)\big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{3\cos(\bar{\alpha})}{(2\pi+\bar{\alpha})^{2}}e^{\tau-2}+\frac{\sin(\bar{\alpha})}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}e^{\tau-3}\frac{(\frac{\tau-3}{\theta}-\frac{1}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}})^{2}}{2}1\!\!\mathrm{I}_{0\geq\tau-3\geq-\frac{\theta}{2\pi+\bar{\alpha}}}\bigg)\Bigg]\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}+o(\theta^{2}).
